-like type would only give us bridges for those relations that factor through the bridge relation on one
endpoint—more generally, through some bridge
\bm{x}.B
we already have in hand.

We only mean in the above to give some intuition for the difference between the affine and structural
situation, not for example to prove beyond a shadow of a doubt that no
\mathsf{Gel}
-like type can exist
structurally. However, we note that in the bisimplicial set semantics of Riehl and Shulman’s directed type
theory
[
RS17
]
, a similar setting, an issue of dimension shift does indeed prevent the existence of a
universe where arrows correspond to relations
[
Rie18
]
.

We now proceed to prove the relativity principle.

Theorem 8
.

For any
{A_{0},A_{1}}\in{\mathcal{U}}
,

{\lambda{C}.{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}}}}\in{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathcal{U}}(A_{0},A_{1})\to(A_{0}\times A_{1}\to\mathcal{U})}
is an
isomorphism.

Proof 2.2
.

As candidate inverse, we of course take
\lambda{R}.{\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},R)}
.

First we show that this is a left inverse,
i.e.
, that the following holds.

({R}{:}{A_{0}\times A_{1}\to\mathcal{U}})\to{\,}\mathsf{Path}_{A_{0}\times A_{1}\to\mathcal{U}}(\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},R)},R)

Let
R:A_{0}\times A_{1}\to\mathcal{U}
be given. We need to construct a path in
A_{0}\times A_{1}\to\mathcal{U}
,
so we apply function extensionality and univalence. Then for every
a_{0}:A_{0}
and
a_{1}:A
, we need an
isomorphism
\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},R)}(a_{0},a_{1})\simeq R\langle a_{0},a_{1}\rangle
. This isomorphism
is implemented exactly by the introduction and elimination forms of the
\mathsf{Gel}
-type, and the inverse
conditions hold (up to exact equality) by
Gel-
\beta
and
Gel-
\eta
.

Now we show it is also a right inverse.

({C}{:}{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathcal{U}}(A_{0},A_{1})})\to{\,}\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathcal{U}}(A_{0},A_{1})}(\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}}),C)

Let
C:\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathcal{U}}(A_{0},A_{1})
be given. We are asked to provide a square with the following
boundary.

\bm{x}
y
A_{0}
A_{1}
A_{0}
A_{1}
\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}})
C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}
A_{0}
A_{1}

By “flipping” this square—
i.e.
, using the correspondence between bridges of paths and paths of bridges
given by exchange of variables—it suffices to show the following.

\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Path}_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}}),C\bm{@}{\bm{x}})}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.A_{0},\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.A_{1})

Now we apply univalence, converting the path type in the universe to a type of isomorphisms. Here we use the
fact that the constant paths
\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.A_{\varepsilon}
correspond to identity isomorphisms
\mathsf{idiso}(A_{\varepsilon})

across univalence. This reduces our goal to the following.

\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}})\simeq C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}}(\mathsf{idiso}(A_{0}),\mathsf{idiso}(A_{1}))

Finally we apply Proposition
7
, reducing the goal once more.

({a_{0}}{:}{A_{0}}){\,}({a_{1}}{:}{A_{1}})\to{\,}\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}})}(a_{0},a_{1})\simeq\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}}(a_{0},a_{1})

This is a consequence of the left inverse condition we have already proven.

Note that the proof of relativity relies on univalence; not surprising, since it is an isomorphism between
types that involve the universe. (It also relies directly on function extensionality, both for paths and
bridges.) In
[
BCM15
]
, which does not include univalence, relativity is instead ensured by imposing
stronger equations on
\mathsf{Gel}
-types—precisely the equations
\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},R)}=R

and
C=\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}})
required for the proof. (These
equations are there named
Pair-Pred
and
Surj-Typ
.) These equations make it more
difficult to construct a presheaf model, as we discuss further in
Section

6
.

2.5.
Using affine variables for paths

Before we dive into using parametric cubical type theory, let us take one more moment to reflect on structural
and substructural interval variables. We have seen why affinity is important for parametric type theory, but
is structurality important for cubical type theory? The Bezem-Coquand-Huber model gives a partial negative
answer: there is a model of univalent type theory in presheaves on the affine cube category
[
BCH13
,
BCH19
]
. While no one has attempted to design a type theory based on this model, it is plausible
that it could be done.

Unfortunately, affine interval variables create problems for modeling higher inductive types. Consider, for
example, the following extremely simple type, which has a single path constructor with no fixed
boundary.

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{data}~{\mathsf{line}}~\mathsf{where}\\
\mid{\mathsf{in}}({x:\mathbb{I}})\in{\mathsf{line}}\\
\end{array}

This specification generates the following elimination principle and computation rule, which essentially says
that maps out of
\mathsf{line}
correspond to terms in a context extended with an interval variable.

\frac{{{\Gamma},{a}:{\mathsf{line}}}\gg{C}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{\Gamma}\gg{M}\in{\mathsf{line}}\quad{{\Gamma},{x}:\mathbb{I}}\gg{Q_{\mathsf{in}}}\in{C[\mathsf{in}(x)/a]}}{{\Gamma}\gg{\mathsf{interval}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{elim}_{a.C}(M,x.Q_{\mathsf{in}})}\in{C[M/a]}}\frac{{{\Gamma},{a}:{\mathsf{line}}}\gg{C}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{\Gamma}\gg{r}\in\mathbb{I}\quad{{\Gamma},{x}:\mathbb{I}}\gg{Q_{\mathsf{in}}}\in{C[\mathsf{in}(x)/a]}}{{\Gamma}\gg{\mathsf{interval}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{elim}_{a.C}(\mathsf{in}(r),x.Q_{\mathsf{in}})}={Q_{\mathsf{in}}[{r}/{x}]}\in{C[\mathsf{in}(r)/a]}}

The issue is in the computation rule, which applies the interval substitution
-[{r}/{x}]
to

Q_{\mathsf{in}}
. If our interval is affine, then this substitution will be nonsensical if
Q_{\mathsf{in}}
already
mentions
r
. Moreover, it is not clear how to restrict the premises of
\mathsf{interval}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{elim}
to ensure the substitution
is sensible without ending up with an insufficiently powerful principle. On a more conceptual level, the

\mathsf{line}
type is suspicious in an affine system in that
structural
maps out of
\mathsf{line}
correspond to

affine
maps out of the interval.

The problem of higher inductive types is one reason why research in cubical type theory and models has shifted
from substructural to structural interval variables. There is also the fact that structural variables are
simply easier to work with. Still, the BCH model does have some intriguing advantages; for one, univalence can
be implemented in
\mathsf{Gel}
-like rather
\mathsf{V}
-like fashion, and the former admits simpler implementations of
coercion and composition.

3.
Applying internal parametricity

Now that we have laid out what we need of parametric cubical type theory, we can get started proving
theorems. We will begin with a classic application of parametricity: relating inductive types to their Church
encodings, in this case booleans.

3.1.
Booleans

The
Church booleans
are the polymorphic binary operators, the elements of the type

\mathbb{B}\coloneqq({A}{:}{\mathcal{U}})\to{\,}A\to A\to A
. Clearly this type has at least two
elements,
\lambda{A}.{\lambda{t}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{t}}}
and
\lambda{A}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{\lambda{f}.{f}}}
. It is a classical
consequence of parametricity that these are the
only
two elements of
\mathbb{B}
. Using internal
parametricity, we can prove that
\mathbb{B}
is indeed isomorphic to the standard type of booleans
(
\mathsf{bool}
).

Theorem 9
.

\mathsf{bool}\simeq\mathbb{B}
.

Proof 3.1
.

It is easy to define functions
{F}\in{\mathsf{bool}\to\mathbb{B}}
and

{G}\in{\mathbb{B}\to\mathsf{bool}}
in either direction.

F\coloneqq\lambda{b}.{\lambda{A}.{\lambda{t}.{\lambda{f}.{\mathsf{if}_{\rule{3.01389pt}{0.4pt}.A}({b};{t},{f})}}}}\qquad G\coloneqq\lambda{k}.{k(\mathsf{bool})(\mathsf{tt})(\mathsf{ff})}

Moreover, it is easy to check by case-analysis that
G(Fb)
is path-equal to
b
for any
b:\mathsf{bool}
.

We use parametricity to prove the other inverse condition. Let some
{k}:{\mathbb{B}}
along
with
{{{A}:{\mathcal{U}}},{t}:{A}},{f}:{A}
be given. We intend to show that
F(Gk)Atf
is
path-equal to
kAtf
. We define a relation
{R}\in{{\mathsf{bool}}\times{A}\to\mathcal{U}}
as follows.

R\langle b,a\rangle\coloneqq\mathsf{Path}_{A}(FbAtf,a)

That is,
R
is the graph of
\lambda{b}.{FbAtf}
. Abstracting a bridge interval variable
\bm{x}
, we can
apply
k
at the
\mathsf{Gel}
-type corresponding to
R
.

{k(\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},A,R))}\in{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},A,R)\to\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},A,R)\to\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},A,R)}

We see that
\mathsf{tt}
and
t
are related by
R
: we have

{\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.t}\in{R\langle\mathsf{tt},t\rangle}
. Likewise, we have

{\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.f}\in{R\langle\mathsf{ff},f\rangle}
. We apply
k
at the two
\mathsf{gel}
terms corresponding to
these witnesses of the relation.

{k(\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},A,R))(\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{tt},t,\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.t))(\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{ff},f,\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.f))}\in{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},A,R)}

If we substitute
\bm{0}
for
\bm{x}
, each
\mathsf{Gel}
and
\mathsf{gel}
term reduces to its first term argument,
leaving
k(\mathsf{bool})(\mathsf{tt})(\mathsf{ff})
, which is
Gk
. Likewise, if we substitute
\bm{1}
, we get

kAtf
. When we bind
\bm{x}
and project the relation witness from this term, we therefore wind up with the
following.

{\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.k(\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},A,R))(\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{tt},t,\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.t))(\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{ff},f,\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.f)))}\in{R\langle Gk,kAtf\rangle}

By definition of
R
, this is exactly our goal: a path from
F(Gk)Atf
to
kAtf
. By function
extensionality, we get a term in
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{B}}(F(Gk),k)
.

This argument follows the shape of a classical parametricity proof: we define a relation, apply a function to
related arguments (here represented by
\mathsf{gel}
terms), and conclude that the outputs are also related (via

\mathsf{ungel}
). We can apply similar arguments to characterize other Church encodings. For example, we can show
that the type
({A}{:}{\mathcal{U}})\to{\,}A\to(A\to A)\to A
is isomorphic to the natural numbers; in that case,
we would also use
\mathsf{extent}
to construct a bridge in the function type.

Note that because the system is predicative, it does not appear possible to simply
define
inductive
types using Church encodings. In the absence of a primitive boolean type in
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathbb{B}
can
only eliminate into small types (that is, types in the universe
\mathcal{U}
). When there
is
a primitive
boolean type, however,
\mathbb{B}
inherits its properties: we can define functions from
\mathbb{B}

into large type by induction by factoring through the map
\mathbb{B}\to\mathsf{bool}
.

The picture gets more complex when we consider Church encodings that are parameterized over “external”
types, such as the following encoding of the coproduct.

A+B\mathrel{\overset{?}{\simeq}}({C}{:}{\mathcal{U}})\to{\,}(A\to C)\to(B\to C)\to C

A classical proof would rely on the
identity extension lemma

[
Rey83
]
, which implies in
particular that the relational interpretation of a closed type (
A
or
B
here) is the identity
relation. This is not the case in BCM-style internal parametricity. In particular, the principle fails for the
universe: the types
\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathcal{U}}(A,B)
and
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathcal{U}}(A,B)
are not the same, as one is
isomorphic to
A\times B\to\mathcal{U}
and the other is isomorphic to
A\simeq B
.

If we focus our attention on small types, we will see that any concrete type
A
we can think of will satisfy

\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(a,b)\simeq\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b)
for all
a,b:A
; however, there is no way to prove for an
arbitrary
A
. We say that types that do satisfy this principle are
bridge-discrete
. We can show that
the universe of bridge-discreteness types is well-behaved and closed under most type formers.

3.2.
Bridge-discrete types

In any type, we have a canonical map from paths to bridges induced by coercion. A type is

bridge-discrete
when this map is an isomorphism.

{defi}

For
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
and
{M,N}\in{A}
, define
{\mathsf{loosen}_{A}}\in{\mathsf{Path}_{A}(M,N)\to\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M,N)}

by
\mathsf{loosen}_{A}\coloneqq\lambda{p}.{\mathsf{coe}_{x.\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(p@0,p@x)}^{0\rightsquigarrow 1}(\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{\rule{4.44444pt}{0.4pt}}.p@0)}
.

Remark 10
.

For any
{M}\in{A}
,
\mathsf{loosen}_{A}
takes the reflexive path on
M
to the reflexive bridge on
A
:
we have
{\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}y.\mathsf{coe}_{x.\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M,M)}^{y\rightsquigarrow 1}(\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{\rule{4.44444pt}{0.4pt}}.M)}\in{\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M,M)}(\mathsf{loosen}_{A}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.M),\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{}.\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}{M})}
.

{defi}

Given
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
, define
{\mathsf{isBDisc}(A)}\ \mathsf{type}
as follows.

\mathsf{isBDisc}(A)\coloneqq({a}{:}{A}){\,}({b}{:}{A})\to{\,}\mathsf{isIso}(\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b),\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(a,b),\mathsf{loosen}_{A})

As we mentioned in
Section

2.4
, the type
\mathsf{isIso}
is always a proposition
[
Uni13
, Theorem
4.3.2]
; any two proofs of
\mathsf{isIso}
are connected by a path. A function type with propositional
codomain is again a proposition
[
Uni13
, Example 2.6.2]
, so
\mathsf{isBDisc}(A)
is a proposition. We
define the
universe of bridge-discrete types
as
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{BDisc}}\coloneqq({A}:{\mathcal{U}})\times\mathsf{isBDisc}(A)
.

Before continuing, we recall some standard results from univalent type theory. The proofs we reference are
conducted using Martin-Löf identity types, but can be readily adapted to cubical path types by way of
Lemma
4
.

Proposition 11
.

Let
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
and let
{{{a}:{A}},{b}:{A}}\gg{R}\ \mathsf{type}
be a relation on
A
. Suppose we
have a family of maps with right inverses:

\triangleright

{F}\in{({a}{:}{A}){\,}({b}{:}{A})\to{\,}R\langle a,b\rangle\to\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b)}
,

\triangleright

{G}\in{({a}{:}{A}){\,}({b}{:}{A})\to{\,}\mathsf{Rinv}(R\langle a,b\rangle,\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b),Fab)}
.

The
Fab
is an isomorphism for all
a,b:A
.

Proof 3.2
.

[
Rij18
, Corollary 1.2.6]
.

Proposition 12
.

Let
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
,
{{a}:{A}}\gg{B_{0},B_{1}}\ \mathsf{type}
, and
{F}\in{({a}{:}{A})\to{\,}B_{0}\to B_{1}}
be
given. Then
{\lambda{\langle a,b\rangle}.{\langle a,Fab\rangle}}\in{(({a}:{A})\times B_{0})\to({a}:{A})\times B_{1}}

is an isomorphism if and only if
Fa
is an isomorphism for all
a:A
.

Proof 3.3
.

[
Uni13
, Theorem 4.7.7]
.

{defi}

A type is
contractible
if it is a proposition and inhabited.

Proposition 13
.

Any function between contractible types is an isomorphism.

Proof 3.4
.

This is an elementary consequence of the definition.

Proposition 14
.

For any
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
and
{M}\in{A}
, the type
({a}:{A})\times\mathsf{Path}_{A}(M,a)
is contractible.

Proof 3.5
.

[
Uni13
, Lemma 3.11.8]
.

Taken together, these results give us a convenient method for showing that a type is bridge-discrete without
reference to
\mathsf{loosen}_{A}
.

Lemma 15
.

Suppose we have a family of maps with right inverses:

\triangleright

{F}\in{({a}{:}{A}){\,}({b}{:}{A})\to{\,}\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(a,b)\to\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b)}
,

\triangleright

{G}\in{({a}{:}{A}){\,}({b}{:}{A})\to{\,}\mathsf{Rinv}(\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(a,b),\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b),Fab)}
.

Then
A
is bridge-discrete. In particular, if
\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(a,b)
and
\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b)
are isomorphic
for all
a,b:A
, then
A
is bridge-discrete.

Proof 3.6
.

By Proposition
11
,
Fab
is an isomorphism for all
a,b:A
. By Proposition
12
, we conclude that
({b}:{A})\times\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(a,b)
and

({b}:{A})\times\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b)
are isomorphic for all
a:A
. The latter is contractible by
Proposition
14
, so the former is contractible as well. Thus

{\lambda{\langle b,p\rangle}.{\langle b,\mathsf{loosen}_{A}(p)\rangle}}\in{(({b}:{A})\times\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b))\to({b}:{A})\times\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(a,b)}
is an isomorphism for all
b:A
, so
A
is bridge-discrete by
Proposition
12
.

Lemma 16
.

Let
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
and
{{a}:{A}}\gg{B}\ \mathsf{type}
be given. If
B
is bridge-discrete for all

{a}:{A}
, then we have the following isomorphism for all
a_{0},a_{1}:A
,
t:B[a_{0}/a]
,

t^{\prime}:B[a_{1}/a]
, and
p:\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a_{0},a_{1})
.

\mathsf{Path}_{x.B[p@x/a]}(t,t^{\prime})\simeq\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.B[\mathsf{loosen}_{A}(p)\bm{@}{\bm{x}}/a]}(t,t^{\prime})

Proof 3.7
.

By Lemma
4
, it suffices to prove the theorem when
a_{1}
is
a_{0}
and
p
is
\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}.\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}{a_{0}}
. In
that case it follows from Remark
10
and the assumption that
B
is bridge-discrete.

Theorem 17
.

Given
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
and
{{a}:{A}}\gg{B}\ \mathsf{type}
, if
A
is bridge-discrete and
B
is bridge-discrete
for all
{a}:{A}
, then
({a}:{A})\times B
is bridge-discrete.

Proof 3.8
.

Given
t,t^{\prime}:({a}:{A})\times B
, we can characterize paths between
t
and
t^{\prime}
as pairs of paths between
their components.

\displaystyle\mathsf{Path}_{({a}:{A})\times B}(t,t^{\prime})\simeq({p}:{\mathsf{Path}_{A}(\mathsf{fst}(t),\mathsf{fst}(t^{\prime}))})\times\mathsf{Path}_{x.B[p@x/a]}(\mathsf{snd}(t),\mathsf{snd}(t^{\prime}))

In the forward direction we have

\lambda{p}.{\langle\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}x.\mathsf{fst}(p@x),\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}x.\mathsf{snd}(p@x)\rangle}
, and in the
reverse we have
\lambda{\langle q_{0},q_{1}\rangle}.{\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}x.\langle q_{0}@x,q_{1}@x\rangle}
; these
are clearly inverses. We can repeat the proof to obtain an analogous characterization of bridges in

({a}:{A})\times B
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{Bridge}_{({a}:{A})\times B}(t,t^{\prime})\simeq({p}:{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(\mathsf{fst}(t),\mathsf{fst}(t^{\prime}))})\times\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.B[p\bm{@}{\bm{x}}/a]}(\mathsf{snd}(t),\mathsf{snd}(t^{\prime}))

By assumption, we know that
\mathsf{Path}_{A}(\mathsf{fst}(t),\mathsf{fst}(t^{\prime}))
and
\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(\mathsf{fst}(t),\mathsf{fst}(t^{\prime}))

are isomorphic via
\mathsf{loosen}_{A}
. To show that the product types are isomorphic, it then suffices to show
the second component types are isomorphic over
\mathsf{loosen}_{A}
,
i.e.
, that the following holds for all

p:\mathsf{Path}_{A}(\mathsf{fst}(t),\mathsf{fst}(t^{\prime}))
.

\mathsf{Path}_{x.B[p@x/a]}(\mathsf{snd}(t),\mathsf{snd}(t^{\prime}))\simeq\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.B[\mathsf{loosen}_{A}(p)\bm{@}{\bm{x}}/a]}(\mathsf{snd}(t),\mathsf{snd}(t^{\prime}))

This is immediate by Lemma
16
.

Theorem 18
.

Given
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
and
{{a}:{A}}\gg{B}\ \mathsf{type}
, if
A
is bridge-discrete and
B
is bridge-discrete
for all
{a}:{A}
, then
({a}{:}{A})\to{\,}B
is bridge-discrete.

Proof 3.9
.

Analogous to Theorem
17
, using Lemmas
3
and
5
.

Theorem 19
.

If
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
is bridge-discrete, then
\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b)
is bridge-discrete for all
a,b:A
.

Proof 3.10
.

Given
p,q:\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b)
, We have the following chain of isomorphisms.

\displaystyle\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b)}(p,q)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathsf{Path}_{x.\mathsf{Path}_{A}(p@x,q@x)}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}.\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}{a},\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}.\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}{b})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathsf{Path}_{x.\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(p@x,q@x)}(\mathsf{loosen}_{A}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}.\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}{a}),\mathsf{loosen}_{A}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}.\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}{b}))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathsf{Path}_{x.\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(p@x,q@x)}(\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{}.\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}{a},\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{}.\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}{b})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathsf{Path}_{A}(a,b)}(p,q)

The first step is by reordering interval abstractions, the second by Remark
10
, the third
by assumption that
A
is bridge-discrete, and the fourth by reordering abstractions again.

Corollary 20
.

If
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
is bridge-discrete, then
\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(a,b)
is bridge-discrete for all
a,b:A
.

Theorem 21
.

\mathsf{bool}
is bridge-discrete.

Proof 3.11
.

We must define a right inverse to

{\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}}\in{\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}}(b,b^{\prime})\to\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathsf{bool}}(b,b^{\prime})}
for every

b,b^{\prime}:\mathsf{bool}
. For simplicity, we prove the case where
b=\mathsf{tt}
and
b^{\prime}=\mathsf{ff}
; the other cases
follow by the same argument. In this case, we first need a function of the following type.

{\mathsf{tighten}}\in{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})\to\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})}

We make use of the type
\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},\mathsf{bool},\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}})
, the bridge from
\mathsf{bool}
to

\mathsf{bool}
corresponding to the path relation. This type has two canonical elements given by reflexivity at

\mathsf{tt}
and
\mathsf{ff}
.

\mathsf{tt}_{\bm{x}}\coloneqq\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{tt},\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\mathsf{tt})\qquad\qquad\mathsf{ff}_{\bm{x}}\coloneqq\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{ff},\mathsf{ff},\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\mathsf{ff})

Given
\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}
, we define an auxiliary function

{\mathsf{tighten}_{\bm{x}}}\in{\mathsf{bool}\to\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},\mathsf{bool},\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}})}
sending each

b:\mathsf{bool}
to the corresponding such element.

\mathsf{tighten}_{\bm{x}}\coloneqq\lambda{b}.{\mathsf{if}_{\rule{3.01389pt}{0.4pt}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool},\mathsf{bool},\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}})}({b};{\mathsf{tt}_{\bm{x}}},{\mathsf{ff}_{\bm{x}}})}

We then define
\mathsf{tighten}\coloneqq\lambda{q}.{\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.\mathsf{tighten}_{\bm{x}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}}))}
, applying

\mathsf{tighten}_{\bm{x}}
pointwise to the input bridge.

To equate
\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{tighten}(q))
with
q
, we need a term as follows.

{\mathsf{inv}}\in{({q}{:}{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})})\to{\,}\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})}(\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{tighten}(q)),q)}

We again begin by defining an auxiliary function
\mathsf{inv}_{\bm{x}}
of the following type.

{\mathsf{inv}_{\bm{x}}}\in{({b}{:}{\mathsf{bool}})\to{\,}\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}}((\mathsf{bridge{\mathchar 45\relax}funext}(\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}\circ\mathsf{tighten})\bm{@}{\bm{x}})(b),b)}

We define
\mathsf{inv}_{\bm{x}}(b)
by induction on
b
. When
b
is
\mathsf{tt}
, we have the following
chain of equalities.

\displaystyle(\mathsf{bridge{\mathchar 45\relax}funext}(\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}\circ\mathsf{tighten})\bm{@}{\bm{x}})(\mathsf{tt})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.\mathsf{tighten}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{tt})))\bm{@}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.\mathsf{tt}_{\bm{x}}))\bm{@}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\mathsf{tt})\bm{@}{\bm{x}}

The first equation is
Extent-
\beta
, the second is by definition of
\mathsf{tighten}_{\bm{x}}
, and the
third is
Gel-
\beta
. Finally,
\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\mathsf{tt})\bm{@}{\bm{x}}
is
path-equal to
\mathsf{tt}
by Remark
10
. The
\mathsf{ff}
case follows by the same argument.
Note that both
{\mathsf{inv}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{tt})}\in{\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{tt})}
and

{\mathsf{inv}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(\mathsf{ff})}\in{\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{ff},\mathsf{ff})}
are reflexive paths for

\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
.

Given
q:\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff})
, we see that the pointwise application

\mathsf{inv}_{\bm{x}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}})@y
fills the following square.

\bm{x}
y
\mathsf{tt}
\mathsf{ff}
\mathsf{tt}
\mathsf{ff}
(\mathsf{bridge{\mathchar 45\relax}funext}(\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}\circ\mathsf{tighten})\bm{@}{\bm{x}})(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}})
q\bm{@}{\bm{x}}
\mathsf{tt}
\mathsf{ff}
\mathsf{inv}_{\bm{x}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}})@y

By
Extent-
\beta
, the top of this square is equal to

\mathsf{loosen}_{\mathsf{bool}}(\mathsf{tighten}(q))\bm{@}{\bm{x}}
. We may therefore define

\mathsf{inv}\coloneqq\lambda{q}.{\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}y.\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.\mathsf{inv}_{\bm{x}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}})@y}
.

The pattern of argument we used for
\mathsf{bool}
generalizes to characterize the bridge types of other inductive
types, and in particular to show that inductive types preserve bridge-discreteness. (We will see something
like it again in
Section

3.4
.) The fact that relativity is used (via
\mathsf{Gel}
-types) in these
proofs is an interesting parallel to the use of univalence to characterize the path types of higher inductive
types (
e.g.
,
[
Uni13
, §8.1]
).

The bridge-discrete types are even closed under
\mathsf{Gel}
-types, which means that we can also carry out
parametricity arguments in
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{BDisc}}
. For example, we can show that the Church encoding

({A}{:}{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{BDisc}}})\to{\,}\mathsf{fst}(A)\to\mathsf{fst}(A)\to\mathsf{fst}(A)
is also isomorphic to
\mathsf{bool}
.

Theorem 22
.

Let
{A_{0},A_{1}}\ \mathsf{type}
and
{{{a_{0}}:{A_{0}}},{a_{1}}:{A_{1}}}\gg{R}\ \mathsf{type}
be given. If
A_{0}
and

A_{1}
are bridge-discrete and
Ra_{0}a_{1}
is bridge-discrete for all
a_{0},a_{1}
, then

\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)
is bridge-discrete for all
\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}
.

Proof 3.12
.

Abbreviate
G_{\bm{x}}\coloneqq\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)
. We show

\mathsf{Path}_{G_{\bm{x}}}(g,g^{\prime})\simeq\mathsf{Bridge}_{G_{\bm{x}}}(g,g^{\prime})
for all
\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}
and

{g,g^{\prime}}\in{G_{\bm{x}}}
. Note that when
\bm{x}
is an endpoint, this holds by the assumptions that
A_{0}
and

A_{1}
are bridge-discrete.

We apply
\mathsf{extent}
at
\bm{x}
, first with
g
and then with
g^{\prime}
. It then remains to show that for all

a_{0},a_{0}^{\prime}:A_{0}
,
a_{1},a_{1}^{\prime}:A_{1}
,
q:\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.G_{\bm{x}}}(a_{0},a_{1})
,

q^{\prime}:\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.G_{\bm{x}}}(a_{0}^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime})
, and
\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}
, we have

\mathsf{Path}_{G_{\bm{x}}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}},q^{\prime}\bm{@}{\bm{x}})\simeq\mathsf{Bridge}_{G_{\bm{x}}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}},q^{\prime}\bm{@}{\bm{x}})
agreeing with the
\mathsf{loosen}_{A}
isomorphism when

\bm{x}=\bm{0}
and
\mathsf{loosen}_{B}
isomorphism when
\bm{x}=\bm{1}
. By Proposition
7
,
it is enough to give an isomorphism

\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Path}_{G_{\bm{x}}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}},q^{\prime}\bm{@}{\bm{x}})}(p_{0},p_{1})\simeq\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Bridge}_{G_{\bm{x}}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}},q^{\prime}\bm{@}{\bm{x}})}(\mathsf{loosen}_{A_{0}}(p_{0}),\mathsf{loosen}_{A_{1}}(p_{1}))

for every
p_{0}:\mathsf{Path}_{A_{0}}(a_{0},a_{0}^{\prime})
and
p_{1}:\mathsf{Path}_{A_{1}}(a_{1},a_{1}^{\prime})
. By identity elimination (Lemma
4
), we may assume that
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
are reflexive paths, in which case (with the help of Remark
10
) we need to show the following for all
q,q^{\prime}:\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.G_{\bm{x}}}(a_{0},a_{1})
.

\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Path}_{G_{\bm{x}}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}},q^{\prime}\bm{@}{\bm{x}})}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.a_{0},\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.a_{1})\simeq\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Bridge}_{G_{\bm{x}}}(q\bm{@}{\bm{x}},q^{\prime}\bm{@}{\bm{x}})}(\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{\rule{4.44444pt}{0.4pt}}.a_{0},\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{\rule{4.44444pt}{0.4pt}}.a_{1})

Now we flip the binders on either side, leaving us to prove the following.

\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.G_{\bm{x}}}(a_{0},a_{1})}(q,q^{\prime})\simeq\mathsf{Bridge}_{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.G_{\bm{x}}}(a_{0},a_{1})}(q,q^{\prime})

In other words, we need to show that
\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.G_{\bm{x}}}(a_{0},a_{1})
is bridge-discrete; this type is
isomorphic to
R
by relativity, so we are finished by assumption.

3.3.
The law of the excluded middle

As a corollary to the bridge-discreteness of
\mathsf{bool}
, we can refute the law of the excluded middle for
propositions. First, let us introduce a few variations on the excluded middle.

\displaystyle\mathsf{LEM}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\coloneqq({A}{:}{\mathcal{U}})\to{\,}({b}:{\mathsf{bool}})\times\mathsf{if}_{\rule{3.01389pt}{0.4pt}.\mathcal{U}}({b};{A},{\lnot{A}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{LEM}_{-1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq({A}{:}{\mathcal{U}})\to{\,}\mathsf{isProp}(A)\to({b}:{\mathsf{bool}})\times\mathsf{if}_{\rule{3.01389pt}{0.4pt}.\mathcal{U}}({b};{A},{\lnot{A}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{WLEM}

\displaystyle\coloneqq({A}{:}{\mathcal{U}})\to{\,}({b}:{\mathsf{bool}})\times\mathsf{if}_{\rule{3.01389pt}{0.4pt}.\mathcal{U}}({b};{\lnot{A}},{\lnot{\lnot{A}}})

The
unrestricted excluded middle
,
\mathsf{LEM}_{\infty}
, is already refuted by univalence
[
Uni13
, Corollary
4.2.7]
. In short, we can obtain a contradiction by examining the action of
\mathsf{LEM}_{\infty}
on the
negation isomorphism
{\mathsf{not}}\in{\mathsf{bool}\simeq\mathsf{bool}}
between
\mathsf{bool}
and itself. In univalent
type theory, it is therefore customary to restrict the law to propositions (Definition
1.4
).
The
excluded middle for propositions
,
\mathsf{LEM}_{-1}
, is validated in the simplicial model of univalent type
theory
[
KL20
]
.

In parametric type theory, however, even this law is refuted. In fact, we can contradict the
weak
excluded middle
,
\mathsf{WLEM}
, which applies only to negated types. It follows from function extensionality
that negated types are always propositions, so we have
\mathsf{LEM}_{-1}\to\mathsf{WLEM}
.

Lemma 23
.

If
{A}\ \mathsf{type}
is bridge-discrete, then any function
{F}\in{\mathcal{U}\to A}
is constant.

Proof 3.13
.

For any pair of types
B_{0},B_{1}
, we can apply
F
at the empty relation between them.

{\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.F(\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(B_{0},B_{1},\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\bot))}\in{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(FB_{0},FB_{1})}

When
A
is bridge-discrete, this induces a path between
FB_{0}
and
FB_{1}
.

Theorem 24
.

\lnot{\mathsf{WLEM}}
.

Proof 3.14
.

Suppose we have
{w}\in{\mathsf{WLEM}}
. By Lemma
23
, we know that
\mathsf{fst}\circ w
is
constant, so
\mathsf{fst}(w\top)
and
\mathsf{fst}(w\bot)
are equal. We obtain a contradiction by case analysis;
clearly
\mathsf{fst}(w\top)
must be
\mathsf{ff}
and
\mathsf{fst}(w\bot)
must be
\mathsf{tt}
.

For a deeper exploration of the relationship between parametricity and the excluded middle, we refer to Booij,
Escardó, Lumsdaine, and Shulman
[
BELS16
]
.

3.4.
The smash product

Now we come to our motivating example: proving coherence laws for the smash product. In this section, we adopt
some conventions for dealing with pointed types, elements of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}\coloneqq({A}:{\mathcal{U}})\times A
. We
give pointed types names like
A_{*},B_{*},\ldots
and write
A,B,\ldots
and
a_{0},b_{0},\ldots
for their first and
second components respectively. Given two pointed types
A_{*},B_{*}
, the type of basepoint-preserving functions
between them is defined as
A_{*}\to B_{*}\coloneqq({f}:{A\to B})\times\mathsf{Path}_{B}(fa_{0},b_{0})
. The identity
function is a basepoint-preserving function
{\langle\lambda{a}.{a},\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.a_{0}\rangle}\in{A_{*}\to A_{*}}
, and
there is a unique pointed constant function
{\langle\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{b_{0}},\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.b_{0}\rangle}\in{A_{*}\to B_{*}}

between any pair of pointed types. The type of pointed functions can itself be made a pointed type

A_{*}\to_{*}B_{*}
by taking the pointed constant function as basepoint, but we will not need this here. As with
types, we write
f_{*}
for basepoint-preserving functions,
f
for the underlying function, and
f_{0}
for the
proof that it preserves the basepoint. Finally, we write
\mathsf{bool}_{*}
for the booleans with basepoint

\mathsf{tt}
.

The underlying type of the smash product is given by the following higher inductive type.

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{data}~{A_{*}\wedge B_{*}}~\mathsf{where}\\
\mid{\llangle a:A,b:B\rrangle}\in{A_{*}\wedge B_{*}}\\
\mid{\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}}\in{A_{*}\wedge B_{*}}\\
\mid{\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}}\in{A_{*}\wedge B_{*}}\\
\mid{\mathsf{spoke^{L}}}({{{b}:{B}},{x}:\mathbb{I}})\in{A_{*}\wedge B_{*}}~[{x=0\hookrightarrow\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}\mid x=1\hookrightarrow\llangle a_{0},b\rrangle}]\\
\mid{\mathsf{spoke^{R}}}({{{a}:{A}},{x}:\mathbb{I}})\in{A_{*}\wedge B_{*}}~[{x=0\hookrightarrow\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}\mid x=1\hookrightarrow\llangle a,b_{0}\rrangle}]\end{array}

In words,
A_{*}\wedge B_{*}
is the ordinary product
A\times B
quotiented by the relation collapsing together
all elements of the form
\langle a_{0},b\rangle
or
\langle a,b_{0}\rangle
. Elements of the former form are identified
with a new “hub” point
\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}
, while elements of the latter are identified with a separate point

\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}
, producing a shape shown in
Figure

7
. We write
A_{*}\wedge_{*}B_{*}
for the smash product
viewed as a pointed type with basepoint
\llangle a_{0},b_{0}\rrangle
.

A
a_{0}
b_{0}
B
~~\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}
~~\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}
\llangle a_{0},b\rrangle
\scriptstyle\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(b,-)

Figure 7.
The smash product of
\langle A,a_{0}\rangle
and
\langle B,b_{0}\rangle

We will begin by focusing on the following theorem.

Theorem 25
.

Any family of pointed functions
({A_{*},B_{*}}{:}{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}})\to{\,}(A_{*}\wedge_{*}B_{*}\to A_{*}\wedge_{*}B_{*})
is
either the polymorphic identity or the polymorphic constant pointed function, up to a path.

In an effort to show we have nothing up our sleeves, we will avoid sweeping gory details—that is, coherence
proofs—under the rug. However, we encourage the reader to focus on the broad strokes of the argument, and as
such we will be less diligent about
explaining
the gory details.

The relations we use in the following will all be graphs of functions. As such, we introduce the following
shorthand notation.

{defi}

Given
f:A\to B
, write
\mathsf{Gr}_{r}(A,B,f)\coloneqq\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A,B,a.b.\mathsf{Path}_{B}(fa,b))
. Given

f_{*}:A_{*}\to B_{*}
, define

\mathsf{Gr}^{*}_{r}(A_{*},B_{*},f_{*})\coloneqq\langle\mathsf{Gr}_{r}(A,B,f),\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{r}}(a_{0},b_{0},f_{0})\rangle\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}
.

We prove a
graph lemma
(Lemma
27
) that relates the smash product of

\mathsf{Gr}^{*}
-types with the action of the smash product on their underlying functions. First, the following two
technical definitions will be handy for concisely filling coherence conditions.

{defi}

[Concatenation by inverse]
Let
{M}\in{A}
,
{r}\in\mathbb{I}
, and
{{x}:\mathbb{I}}\gg{N}\in{A}
with

{{r=1}}\gg{M}={N[{1}/{x}]}\in{A}
be given. For any
{s}\in\mathbb{I}
, define

{\mathsf{conc{\mathchar 45\relax}inv}_{A}^{r,s}(M,x.N)}\in{A}
as follows.

\mathsf{conc{\mathchar 45\relax}inv}_{A}^{r,s}(M,x.N)\coloneqq\mathsf{hcom}_{A}^{1\rightsquigarrow s}(M;r=0\hookrightarrow\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.M,r=1\hookrightarrow x.N)

r
s
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
M
M
N[s/x]
\mathsf{conc{\mathchar 45\relax}inv}_{A}^{r,s}(M,x.N)

The term
\mathsf{conc{\mathchar 45\relax}inv}_{A}^{r,0}(M,x.N)
is the result of concatenating
M
(as a path in direction
r
) with
the inverse of
x.N
; we need the general form
\mathsf{conc{\mathchar 45\relax}inv}_{A}^{r,s}(M,x.N)
to relate the composite to
other terms.

Lemma 26

(Join connection)
.

For any
{P}\in{\mathsf{Path}_{A}(M,N)}
, we have a term as follows.

{\mathsf{connect}_{A}(P)}\in{\mathsf{Path}_{x.\mathsf{Path}_{A}(P@x,N)}(P,\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.N)}

x
y
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
P@x
N
P@y
N
\mathsf{connect}_{A}(P)@x@y

Proof 3.15
.

By Lemma
4
, it suffices to construct a term when
P
is a constant path

{\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.M}\in{\mathsf{Path}_{A}(M,M)}
, in which case we have

{\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.M}\in{\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{Path}_{A}(M,M)}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.M,\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.M)}
.

The smash product has a functorial action on pointed functions, which we define as follows.

{defi}

Given
f_{*}:A_{*}\to C_{*}
and
g_{*}:B_{*}\to D_{*}
, we inductively define a map

f_{*}\wedge g_{*}\in A_{*}\wedge B_{*}\to C_{*}\wedge D_{*}
as follows.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}(f_{*}\wedge g_{*})(\llangle a,b\rrangle)&\coloneqq&\llangle fa,gb\rrangle\\
(f_{*}\wedge g_{*})(\mathsf{\circledast^{L}})&\coloneqq&\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}\\
(f_{*}\wedge g_{*})(\mathsf{\circledast^{R}})&\coloneqq&\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}\\
(f_{*}\wedge g_{*})(\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(b,y))&\coloneqq&\mathsf{conc{\mathchar 45\relax}inv}_{C_{*}\wedge D_{*}}^{y,0}(\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(gb,y),z.\llangle f_{0}@z,gb\rrangle)\\
(f_{*}\wedge g_{*})(\mathsf{spoke^{R}}(a,y))&\coloneqq&\mathsf{conc{\mathchar 45\relax}inv}_{C_{*}\wedge D_{*}}^{y,0}(\mathsf{spoke^{R}}(y,fa),z.\llangle fa,g_{0}@z\rrangle)\end{array}

We now prove the graph lemma: that there is a map from the smash product of two
\mathsf{Gr}^{*}
-types to the

\mathsf{Gr}
-type corresponding to the smash of their underlying functions. We expect that this map is in fact an
isomorphism and that a similar principle holds for
\mathsf{Gel}
-types more generally, but such results are not
necessary here.

Lemma 27

(Graph Lemma for
\wedge
)
.

For any
{\bm{r}}\in\mathbf{I}
, there is a map

\wedge\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{graph}_{\bm{r}}\in\mathsf{Gr}^{*}_{\bm{r}}(A_{*},C_{*},f_{*})\wedge\mathsf{Gr}^{*}_{\bm{r}}(B_{*},D_{*},g_{*})\to\mathsf{Gr}_{\bm{r}}(A_{*}\wedge B_{*},C_{*}\wedge D_{*},f_{*}\wedge g_{*})

equal to the identity function on
A_{*}\wedge_{*}B_{*}
when
\bm{r}=\bm{0}
and on
C_{*}\wedge_{*}D_{*}
when

\bm{r}=\bm{1}
.

Proof 3.16
.

We define the map by induction on the smash product in the domain.

\triangleright

Case
\llangle m,n\rrangle
: We test whether
\bm{r}
is a constant or variable using
\mathsf{extent}
. In the
constant cases, we return
\llangle m,n\rrangle
. In the case
\bm{r}
is a variable
\bm{x}
, we learn that

m
and
n
are the instantiation at
\bm{x}
of bridges over their types; by
Gel-
\eta
, they
are of the form
m=\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(a,c,p)
and
n=\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(b,d,q)
. We return

\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\llangle a,b\rrangle,\llangle c,d\rrangle,\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}z.\llangle p@y,q@y\rrangle)
.

\triangleright

Case
\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}
: We return
\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{r}}(\mathsf{\circledast^{L}},\mathsf{\circledast^{L}},\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\mathsf{\circledast^{L}})
.

\triangleright

Case
\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}
: Symmetric to
\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}
.

\triangleright

Case
\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(n,y)
: We test whether
\bm{r}
is a constant or variable using
\mathsf{extent}
. In the
constant cases, we return
\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(n,y)
. In the case
\bm{r}
is a variable
\bm{x}
, we learn that

n
is the instantiation at
\bm{x}
of a bridge; by
Gel-
\eta
, it is of the form

n=\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(b,d,q)
. We return
\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(b,y),\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(d,y),\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}z.\cdots)
,
where
\cdots
is the following composite.

\mathsf{hcom}_{C_{*}\wedge D_{*}}^{1\rightsquigarrow 0}\left(\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(q@z,y);\begin{array}[]{lcl}y=0&\hookrightarrow&\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}\\
y=1&\hookrightarrow&w.\llangle\mathsf{connect}_{A}(f_{0})@z@w,q@z\rrangle\\
z=0&\hookrightarrow&w.\mathsf{conc{\mathchar 45\relax}inv}_{C_{*}\wedge D_{*}}^{y,w}(\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(gb,y),z.\llangle f_{0}@z,gb\rrangle)\\
z=1&\hookrightarrow&\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(d,y)\end{array}\right)

\triangleright

Case
\mathsf{spoke^{R}}(m,y)
: Symmetric to
\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(n,y)
.

When
\bm{r}
is a constant, the resulting function simplifies to the
\eta
-expansion of the identity
function on
A_{*}\wedge B_{*}
. By a simple induction on
A_{*}\wedge B_{*}
, the
\eta
-expansion is path-equal
to the identity function. We may therefore apply an
\mathsf{hcom}
to adjust the boundary and obtain a function
that is exactly the identity when
\bm{r}=\bm{0}
or
\bm{r}=\bm{1}
.

Finally, we use the fact that
\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{bool}_{*}
. This is a consequence
of more general facts—that
\mathsf{bool}_{*}
is a unit for the smash product, or alternatively that

(1+X)\wedge(1+Y)\simeq 1+(X\times Y)
when we take
1
for each basepoint—but we prove the
special case directly for simplicity’s sake. The importance of
\mathsf{bool}_{*}
arises from the fact that elements
of a pointed type
X_{*}
are in correspondence with pointed maps
\mathsf{bool}_{*}\to X_{*}
. As such, we can use
naturality conditions with respect to functions
\mathsf{bool}_{*}\to X_{*}
to “probe” the behavior of a function
polymorphic in pointed types, as we will see in Lemma
29
.

Lemma 28

(Smash of booleans)
.

\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{bool}_{*}
; in particular, any element of

\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}
is path-equal to either
\llangle\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{tt}\rrangle
or

\llangle\mathsf{ff},\mathsf{ff}\rrangle
.

Proof 3.17
.

In one direction, we define
{F}\in{\mathsf{bool}\to\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}}
to send
\mathsf{tt}
to

\llangle\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{tt}\rrangle
and
\mathsf{ff}
to
\llangle\mathsf{ff},\mathsf{ff}\rrangle
. In the other, we define

{G}\in{\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}\to\mathsf{bool}}
to send
\llangle\mathsf{ff},\mathsf{ff}\rrangle
to
\mathsf{ff}
and
all other constructors to
\mathsf{tt}
. Clearly
G\circ F
is the identity. For the other inverse condition,
we show
({s}{:}{\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}})\to{\,}\mathsf{Path}_{\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}}(s,F(Gs))
by smash
product induction as follows.

\triangleright

Case
\llangle\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{tt}\rrangle
: Reflexivity.

\triangleright

Case
\llangle\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff}\rrangle
:

\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}y.\mathsf{hcom}_{\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}}^{0\rightsquigarrow 1}(\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(\mathsf{tt},y);y=0\hookrightarrow x.\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(\mathsf{ff},x),y=1\hookrightarrow\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\llangle\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{tt}\rrangle)
.

\triangleright

Case
\llangle\mathsf{ff},\mathsf{ff}\rrangle
: Reflexivity.

\triangleright

Case
\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}
:
\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}y.\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(\mathsf{tt},y)
.

\triangleright

Case
\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(\mathsf{tt},x)
:
\mathsf{connect}_{\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}}(\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}y.\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(\mathsf{tt},y))@x
.

\triangleright

Case
\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(\mathsf{ff},x)
:

\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}y.\mathsf{hcom}_{\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}}^{0\rightsquigarrow x}(\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(\mathsf{tt},y);y=0\hookrightarrow x.\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(\mathsf{ff},x),y=1\hookrightarrow\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.\llangle\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{tt}\rrangle)
.

The cases for
\llangle\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{ff}\rrangle
,
\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}
, and
\mathsf{spoke^{R}}
are obtained by taking the
cases for
\llangle\mathsf{ff},\mathsf{tt}\rrangle
,
\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}
, and
\mathsf{spoke^{L}}
respectively and replacing

\mathsf{spoke^{L}}
with
\mathsf{spoke^{R}}
everywhere.

The following result, which characterizes terms

{F}\in{({A_{*},B_{*}}{:}{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}})\to{\,}A\to B\to A_{*}\wedge B_{*}}
, is the linchpin of the argument; all uses
of internal parametricity in the final results factor through this lemma. As we only use internal
parametricity with relations that are graphs of functions, this result may also be cast as a corollary of the

naturality
of such terms, a special case of parametricity. In particular, we use the following
naturality square for
a:A
and
b:B
, where
{{[c]}_{*}}\in{\mathsf{bool}_{*}\to C_{*}}
is the pointed function
sending
\mathsf{tt}
to
c_{0}
and
\mathsf{ff}
to
c
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{bool}\times\mathsf{bool}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{*}\wedge B_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\mathsf{bool}_{*}\mathsf{bool}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[a]\times[b]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[a]}_{*}\wedge{[b]}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA_{*}B_{*}}

Lemma 29

(Workhorse lemma)
.

Let
{F}\in{({A_{*},B_{*}}{:}{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}})\to{\,}A\to B\to A_{*}\wedge B_{*}}
. Then
F
is path equal to one of the
following.

\triangleright

\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{\lambda{a}.{\lambda{b}.{\llangle a,b\rrangle}}}}
.

\triangleright

\lambda{A_{*}}.{\lambda{B_{*}}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{\llangle a_{0},b_{0}\rrangle}}}}
.

Proof 3.18
.

We show that the identity of
F
is determined by the value of

F(\mathsf{bool}_{*})(\mathsf{bool}_{*})(\mathsf{ff})(\mathsf{ff})
. Let
A_{*}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}
,
B_{*}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}
,
a:A
, and

b:B
be given.

We have a function
{{[a]}_{*}}\in{\mathsf{bool}_{*}\to A_{*}}
sending
\mathsf{tt}
to
a_{0}
and
\mathsf{ff}
to
a
,
likewise
{{[b]}_{*}}\in{\mathsf{bool}_{*}\to B_{*}}
sending
\mathsf{tt}
to
b_{0}
and
\mathsf{ff}
to
b
. Abstract a
bridge variable
\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}
. We abbreviate
G^{a}_{*}\coloneqq\mathsf{Gr}^{*}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool}_{*},A_{*},{[a]}_{*})
and

G^{b}_{*}\coloneqq\mathsf{Gr}^{*}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{bool}_{*},B_{*},{[b]}_{*})
. Applying
F
at
G^{a}_{*}
and
G^{b}_{*}
, we have the
following.

{FG^{a}_{*}G^{b}_{*}(\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{ff},a,\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.a))(\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(\mathsf{ff},b,\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}.b))}\in{G^{a}_{*}\wedge G^{b}_{*}}

At
\bm{x}=\bm{0}
, this term is
F(\mathsf{bool}_{*})(\mathsf{bool}_{*})(\mathsf{ff})(\mathsf{ff})
, and at
\bm{x}=\bm{1}

it is
FA_{*}B_{*}ab
. Now we apply the Graph Lemma to obtain a term in

\mathsf{Gr}_{x}(\mathsf{bool}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{bool}_{*},A_{*}\wedge B_{*},{[a]}_{*}\wedge{[b]}_{*})
with the same boundary. Finally,
we apply
\mathsf{ungel}
to extract a path from

({[a]}_{*}\wedge{[b]}_{*})(F(\mathsf{bool}_{*})(\mathsf{bool}_{*})(\mathsf{ff})(\mathsf{ff}))
to
FA_{*}B_{*}ab
. We therefore see that

F
is the pairing function if
F(\mathsf{bool}_{*})(\mathsf{bool}_{*})(\mathsf{ff})(\mathsf{ff})
is

\llangle\mathsf{ff},\mathsf{ff}\rrangle
and the constant function if it is
\llangle\mathsf{tt},\mathsf{tt}\rrangle
; by Lemma
28
, we are in one of these two cases.

Corollary 30
.

({A_{*},B_{*}}{:}{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}})\to{\,}A\to B\to A_{*}\wedge B_{*}
is a
set
: any pair of paths between two
elements of the type are path-equal.

Proof 3.19
.

Lemma
29
shows that the type is isomorphic to
\mathsf{bool}
, which is a set.

This is everything we need to prove the final result.

Proof 3.20

(Proof of Theorem
25
)
.

Let
{F_{*}}\in{({A_{*},B_{*}}{:}{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}})\to{\,}A_{*}\wedge_{*}B_{*}\to A_{*}\wedge_{*}B_{*}}
be given. To
characterize
F_{*}
, we need to characterize its behavior on each constructor of
A_{*}\wedge B_{*}
as well as
the proof that it preserves the basepoint of
A_{*}\wedge_{*}B_{*}
.

First, by Lemma
29
, we know that
\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}a.\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}b.FA_{*}B_{*}(\llangle a,b\rrangle)

is either pairing or constant. The values of
FA_{*}B_{*}\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}
and
FA_{*}B_{*}\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}
must be path-equal
to
\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}
and
\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}
respectively, as
F
is basepoint-preserving and
\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}

(
\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}
) is connected to the basepoint by
\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(b_{0},-)
(
\mathsf{spoke^{R}}(a_{0},-)
).

Next, observe that we can capture the behavior of
F
on
\mathsf{spoke^{L}}
by the following term, which is a path
in
({A_{*},B_{*}}{:}{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}})\to{\,}A\to B\to A_{*}\wedge_{*}B_{*}
between

\lambda{A_{*}}.{\lambda{B_{*}}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{FA_{*}B_{*}\mathsf{\circledast^{L}}}}}}
and

\lambda{A_{*}}.{\lambda{B_{*}}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{\lambda{b}.{FA_{*}B_{*}(\llangle a,b\rrangle)}}}}
.

\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}y.\lambda{A_{*}}.{\lambda{B_{*}}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{\lambda{b}.{FA_{*}B_{*}(\mathsf{spoke^{L}}(b,y))}}}}

By Corollary
30
, this path is path-equal to any other path in this type, in
particular path-equal to whatever we need it to be to complete this proof. The same applies to

\mathsf{\circledast^{R}}
. Finally, we can apply the same trick for the basepoint path, writing it as a path in the type
from Corollary
30
as follows.

\lambda^{\mathbb{I}}y.\lambda{A_{*}}.{\lambda{B_{*}}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{\lambda{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.4pt}}.{f_{0}A_{*}B_{*}@y}}}}

Now we argue that this strategy can be used to prove the
n
-ary generalization in a uniform way. (The binary
version is in fact not very useful on its own; the direct proof of commutativity for the smash product is
uncharacteristically straightforward, because the definition of
\wedge
is completely symmetric.)

Theorem 31
.

Any function

({A^{0}_{*},\ldots,A^{n}_{*}}{:}{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}})\to{\,}(A^{0}_{*}\wedge_{*}\cdots\wedge_{*}A^{n}_{*})\to(A^{0}_{*}\wedge_{*}\cdots\wedge_{*}A^{n}_{*})
(associating
\wedge_{*}
to the right) is either the polymorphic identity or the
polymorphic constant pointed function.

Proof 3.21
.

We show by induction on
i\leq n+1
that any

({A^{0}_{*},\ldots,A^{n}_{*}}{:}{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{pt}}})\to{\,}A^{0}\to\cdots\to A^{n-i}\to(A^{n-i+1}_{*}\wedge_{*}\cdots\wedge_{*}A^{n}_{*})\to(A^{0}_{*}\wedge_{*}\cdots\wedge_{*}A^{n}_{*})

is either given by iterated pairing or constant. For
i=0
, it follows from a simple
n
-ary
generalization of the workhorse lemma (instantiating each type argument with a graph and applying the binary
Graph Lemma repeatedly). For
i>0
, it follows from the induction hypothesis by the same argument as in
the proof of Theorem
25
.

The key here is that we are never involved in an iterated induction on smash products: for each
i
in the
proof of Theorem
31
, we have an argument by induction on one occurrence of the smash
product, but these arguments do not overlap.

4.
Computational interpretation

We now develop the computational interpretation underlying parametric cubical type theory, building on the
work of Allen for Martin-Löf type theory
[
All87
]
and Angiuli
et al.
for cartesian cubical type theory
[
AFH18
]
. We closely follow the presentation in Angiuli’s thesis
[
Ang19
]
; we will give a
reasonably complete tour through the definitions, but rely on
[
Ang19
]
for many results that are
essentially unaffected by the addition of bridge intervals and parametricity primitives.

An interpretation in these frameworks is built from two components: a deterministic
operational
semantics
on closed untyped terms and a
value type system
. The former explains the evaluation of
terms; the latter explains which closed values are names for types and which closed values are elements of
said types. Given these two components, we derive an interpretation of the open judgments—
{\Gamma}\gg{A}\ \mathsf{type}

and so on—by extending the value type system first to arbitrary closed terms (roughly, a term is well-typed
when it evaluates to a well-typed value) and then to open terms (an open term is well-typed when its closed
instances are well-typed).

4.1.
Interval contexts and terms

In the above and the following,
closed
refers to terms that do not contain term variables but that may
contain interval variables. It is essential to consider evaluation of terms containing interval variables in
order to accommodate the terms
\mathsf{coe}_{x.A}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(M)
and
\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.N)
, which evaluate terms (here
A
and

N
) under interval binders. We use
\Psi
to denote contexts consisting solely of path and bridge interval
variables.

\Psi\Coloneqq\cdot\mid{\Psi},{x}:\mathbb{I}\mid{\Psi},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}

We write
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
for interval substitutions, which take terms
M
in context
\Psi
to
terms
{M}{\psi}
in context
\Psi^{\prime}
. As always, path interval variables are structural and bridge
interval variables are affine;
\psi
cannot identify two bridge variables except by sending both to
\bm{0}
or

\bm{1}
.

{defi}

The path interval term judgment
{\Psi}\Vdash{r}\in\mathbb{I}
is defined to hold when either
r\in\{0,1\}
or
r=x

where
(x:\mathbb{I})\in\Psi
; the bridge interval term judgment
{\Psi}\Vdash{\bm{r}}\in\mathbf{I}
is defined likewise. The
interval substitution judgment is then inductively generated by the following rules.

\frac{}{{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\cdot}\in{\cdot}}\frac{{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}\quad{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{r}\in\mathbb{I}}{{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{({\psi},{r}/{x})}\in{({\Psi},{x}:\mathbb{I})}}\frac{{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\bm{r}}\in\mathbf{I}\quad{{\Psi^{\prime}}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}}{{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{({\psi},{\bm{r}}/{\bm{x}})}\in{({\Psi},\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}}

The judgment
{\Psi}\Vdash{\xi}\ \mathsf{constraint}
is likewise inductively generated by constraints of the form

{\Psi}\Vdash{(r=s)}\ \mathsf{constraint}
and
{\Psi}\Vdash{(\bm{r}=\bm{\varepsilon})}\ \mathsf{constraint}
.

Remark 32
.

We have an operator
\forall\bm{x}.-
on constraints defined as follows.

\displaystyle\forall\bm{x}.(r=s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(r=s)

\displaystyle\forall\bm{x}.(\bm{x}=\bm{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\bm{0}=\bm{1})

\displaystyle\forall\bm{x}.(\bm{r}=\bm{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\bm{r}=\bm{\varepsilon})\quad\text{if $\bm{r}\neq\bm{x}$}

4.2.
Operational semantics

An
operational semantics
, which specifies the evaluation of closed terms, is defined by judgments

M\ \mathsf{val}
(“
M
is a value”) and
M\longmapsto M^{\prime}
(“
M
steps to
M^{\prime}
”) operating on closed terms. We write

M\longmapsto^{*}M^{\prime}
to mean that
M
steps to
M^{\prime}
in zero or more steps and
M\Downarrow V
to mean that

M\longmapsto^{*}V
for some
V\ \mathsf{val}
.

We give the defining rules for our operational semantics in
Figure

8
. We show only those rules that
involve the parametricity primitives; for everything else, we refer to
[
Ang19
, §4.1]
. Although we
choose a specific operational semantics here, the interpretation goes through for any operational semantics
that extends it; we need only the presence of these rules, not the absence of others.

\frac{}{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.A}(M_{0},M_{1})\ \mathsf{val}}\frac{}{\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.P\ \mathsf{val}}\frac{Q\longmapsto Q^{\prime}}{Q\bm{@}{\bm{r}}\longmapsto Q^{\prime}\bm{@}{\bm{r}}}\frac{}{(\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.P)\bm{@}{\bm{r}}\longmapsto P[{\bm{r}}/{\bm{x}}]}\frac{}{\mathsf{hcom}_{\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.A}(M_{0},M_{1})}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(M;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.N_{i}}})\longmapsto\\

\raisebox{-8.00003pt}{$\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.\mathsf{hcom}_{A}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(M\bm{@}{\bm{x}};\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow A_{\varepsilon}}\frac{}{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)\ \mathsf{val}}\frac{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}}{\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(M_{0},M_{1},P)\longmapsto M_{\varepsilon}}\frac{}{\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0},M_{1},P)\ \mathsf{val}}\frac{Q\longmapsto Q^{\prime}}{\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q)\longmapsto\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q^{\prime})}\frac{}{\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0},M_{1},P))\longmapsto P}\frac{M_{\varepsilon,y}\coloneqq\mathsf{hcom}_{A_{\varepsilon}}^{r\rightsquigarrow y}(Q[{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}}];\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}[{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}}]\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}[{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}}]}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}[{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}}]\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}[{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}}]}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}[{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}}]\hookrightarrow y.\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q_{i})}}{\overarrow@\to{\forall\bm{x}.\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q_{i})}}{\overarrow@\to{\forall\bm{x}.\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow
