Figure 8.
Operational semantics of parametric cubical type theory

4.3.
Judgments from a value type system

A
value type system
specifies the values that are names for types and the values that each such type
classifies. For practical purposes, it useful to first introduce
candidate value type systems
and then
impose additional conditions under which a candidate is an actual type system.

{defi}

A
candidate value type system

\tau
is a quaternary relation
\tau(\Psi,V,V^{\prime},\varphi)
ranging over contexts

\Psi
, values
V,V^{\prime}
in context
\Psi
, and binary relations
\varphi
on values in context
\Psi
.

We read an instance
\tau(\Psi,V,V^{\prime},\varphi)
of the relation as specifying that (1) the values
V
and
V^{\prime}
are
equal types in context
\Psi
and that (2) these type names stand for the relation
\varphi
: values
W
and
W^{\prime}

are equal elements of
V
(likewise
V^{\prime}
) in context
\Psi
when
\varphi(W,W^{\prime})
holds.

Given a candidate value type system, we derive
candidate judgments
extending the defining relations to
non-value terms. In
[
All87
]
, a term is a type (resp. well-typed) when it evaluates to a type value
(resp. well-typed value). In a setting with interval variables, it becomes necessary to require a stronger
“coherent evaluation” condition: to be well-typed, a term must not merely evaluate to a well-typed value,
but do so in a way that interacts in a sensible way with interval substitutions. First, we define
“incoherent” extensions of value type systems and terms to terms.

{defi}

Given a candidate value type system, we write
{\tau}^{\Downarrow}(\Psi,A,A^{\prime},\varphi)
for (possibly non-value) terms
A,A^{\prime}

to mean that
A\Downarrow V
and
A^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime}
for some
V,V^{\prime}
with
\tau(\Psi,V,V^{\prime},\varphi)
. Given a relation

\varphi
on values, we define a relation
{\varphi}^{\Downarrow}
on terms:
{\varphi}^{\Downarrow}(M,M^{\prime})
holds when

M\Downarrow V
and
M^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime}
for some
V,V^{\prime}
with
\varphi(V,V^{\prime})
.

To cut down to the coherently well-behaved types and terms, we introduce a notion of
\Psi
-relation
, a
family of relations indexed by the substitutions into
\Psi
.

{defi}

A
\Psi
-relation
\alpha
is a family of binary relations
\alpha_{\psi}
, indexed by substitutions

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
into
\Psi
and where each
\alpha_{\psi}
relates terms in context

\Psi^{\prime}
. Given a
\Psi
-relation
\alpha
and
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
, we define a
\Psi^{\prime}
-relation

{\alpha}{\psi}
by
{({\alpha}{\psi})}_{\psi^{\prime}}\coloneqq\alpha_{\psi\psi^{\prime}}
.

We now define the coherent
candidate judgments
:
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\sim{A^{\prime}}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
, which asserts
that
A
and
A^{\prime}
coherently evaluate to equal type names standing for the
\Psi
-relation
\alpha
, and

{\Psi}\Vdash{M}\sim{M^{\prime}}\in{\alpha}
, which asserts that
M
and
M^{\prime}
coherently evaluate to values equal in
\alpha
.

{defi}

We define the candidate judgments as follows.

\triangleright

{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\sim{A^{\prime}}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
holds when for every
{\Psi_{1}}\Vdash{\psi_{1}}\in{\Psi}
and

{\Psi_{2}}\Vdash{\psi_{2}}\in{\Psi_{1}}
, we have

(1)

{A}{\psi_{1}}\Downarrow A_{1}
and
{A^{\prime}}{\psi_{1}}\Downarrow A_{1}^{\prime}
for some
A_{1},A_{1}^{\prime}
,

(2)

there is some
\varphi
such that
{\tau}^{\Downarrow}(\Psi_{2},-,-,\varphi)
relates

({A_{1}}{\psi_{2}},{A}{\psi_{1}\psi_{2}})
and its reverse,

({A_{1}^{\prime}}{\psi_{2}},{A^{\prime}}{\psi_{1}\psi_{2}})
and its reverse, and

({A_{1}}{\psi_{2}},{A^{\prime}_{1}}{\psi_{2}})
,

and
\alpha
is a
\Psi
-relation on values such that

{\tau}^{\Downarrow}(\Psi^{\prime},{A}{\psi},{A^{\prime}}{\psi},\alpha_{\psi})
for all

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
.

\triangleright

{\Psi}\Vdash{M}\sim{M^{\prime}}\in{\alpha}
holds when for every
{\Psi_{1}}\Vdash{\psi_{1}}\in{\Psi}
and

{\Psi_{2}}\Vdash{\psi_{2}}\in{\Psi_{1}}
, we have

(1)

{M}{\psi_{1}}\Downarrow M_{1}
and
{M^{\prime}}{\psi_{1}}\Downarrow M_{1}^{\prime}
for some
M_{1},M_{1}^{\prime}
,

(2)

{(\alpha_{\psi_{1}\psi_{2}})}^{\Downarrow}
relates
({M_{1}}{\psi_{2}},{M}{\psi_{1}\psi_{2}})
and
its reverse,
({M_{1}^{\prime}}{\psi_{2}},{M^{\prime}}{\psi_{1}\psi_{2}})
and its reverse, and

({M_{1}}{\psi_{2}},{M^{\prime}_{1}}{\psi_{2}})
.

The conditions in the definition of
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\sim{A^{\prime}}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
, for example, ask that we have the
square shown below: whether we apply
\psi_{2}
to
A\psi_{1}
or first evaluate and then apply
\psi_{2}
, we get
the same result up to the equality defined by
{\tau}^{\Downarrow}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\psi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\psi_{1}\psi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\psi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau}^{\Downarrow}}

Note that the candidate judgments are stable under interval substitution by definition: for example, if

{\Psi}\Vdash{M}\sim{M^{\prime}}\in{\alpha}
, then
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{M\psi}\sim{M^{\prime}\psi}\in{\alpha\psi}
for any

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
.

A candidate is a value type system when the typing relation satisfies several additional conditions, which
require that each type names at most one relation, that the type and element relations are partial equivalence
relations, and that any value type is
coherently
a type.

{defi}

A
value type system

\tau
is a candidate value type system satisfying the following.

Unicity:

If
\tau(\Psi,V,V^{\prime},\varphi)
and
\tau(\Psi,V,V^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
, then
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}
.

PER:

\tau(\Psi,-,-,\varphi)
is a partial equivalence relation (PER) for all
\Psi,\varphi
.

PER-valuation:

If
\tau(\Psi,V,V^{\prime},\varphi)
, then
\varphi
is a PER.

Value-coherence:

If
\tau(\Psi,V,V^{\prime},\varphi)
, then
{\Psi}\Vdash{V}\sim{V^{\prime}}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
for some
\alpha
.

Likewise, we will require that the values related by the relations associated to types are in fact coherently
related.

{defi}

We say a
\Psi
-relation
\alpha
is
value-coherent
and write
\mathsf{Coh}(\alpha)
if
\alpha_{\psi}(V,V^{\prime})

implies
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{{V}{\psi}}\sim{{V^{\prime}}{\psi}}\in{{\alpha}{\psi}}
for all

\psi
and
V,V^{\prime}
.

Given a value type system, we obtain typing judgments first on closed and then on open terms. For types, we
also distinguish between
pretypes
and
types
, the latter of which are required to support Kan
operations. For the following series of definitions, we fix an ambient value type system
\tau
.

{defi}

We define the closed judgments as follows.

\triangleright

{\Psi}\Vdash{A}={A^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
holds when
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\sim{A^{\prime}}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
for some value-coherent

\alpha
.

\triangleright

Presupposing
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}={A}\ \mathsf{pretype}
,
{\Psi}\Vdash{M}={M^{\prime}}\in{A}
holds when

{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\sim{A}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
with
{\Psi}\Vdash{M}\sim{M^{\prime}}\in{\alpha}
.

We define
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\ \mathsf{pretype}
to mean
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}={A}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, likewise
{\Psi}\Vdash{M}\in{A}
to mean

{\Psi}\Vdash{M}={M}\in{A}
. We will abbreviate future reflexive judgments in this fashion without comment. When we
have
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, we write
\llbracket{A}\rrbracket
for the (necessarily unique) value
\Psi
-relation assigned to

A
by the value type system.

We now extend the closed judgments to
open judgments
, defined on terms containing arbitrary variables.
We do so by means of a
context instantiation judgment

{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
, which specifies
the ways a general context
\Gamma
may be instantiated by closed terms over
\Psi
.

{defi}

We define the context instantiations
{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
inductively as follows.

\triangleright

{\Psi}\Vdash{\cdot}={\cdot}\in{\cdot}
.

\triangleright

{\Psi}\Vdash{({\gamma},{M}/{a})}={({\gamma^{\prime}},{M^{\prime}}/{a})}\in{({\Gamma},{a}:{A})}
when

{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
and
{\Psi}\Vdash{M}={M^{\prime}}\in{A\gamma}
.

\triangleright

{\Psi}\Vdash{({\gamma},{r}/{x})}={({\gamma},{r}/{x})}\in{({\Gamma},{x}:\mathbb{I})}
when

{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
and
{\Psi}\Vdash{r}\in\mathbb{I}
.

\triangleright

{\Psi}\Vdash{({\gamma},{\bm{r}}/{\bm{x}})}={({\gamma},{\bm{r}}/{\bm{x}})}\in{({\Gamma},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I})}
when

{\Psi}\Vdash{\bm{r}}\in\mathbf{I}
and
{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
.

\triangleright

{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{({\Gamma},{\xi})}
when
{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
and
\xi\gamma
is
true.

The open type and term judgments are then defined to hold when their closed instantiations hold.

{defi}

We define the open judgments as follows.

\triangleright

{\Gamma}\gg{A}={A^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
holds when

{\Psi}\Vdash{{A}{\gamma}}={{A^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
for
all
{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
.

\triangleright

{\Gamma}\gg{M}={M^{\prime}}\in{A}
holds when

{\Psi}\Vdash{{M}{\gamma}}={{M^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}}\in{{A}{\gamma}}

for all
{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
.

We note that, in contrast, we define the open
interval
judgments without reference to the terms in the
context
\Gamma
. It is therefore not the case that, for example,
{{v}:{\bot}}\gg{0}={1}\in\mathbb{I}
; interval
judgments are prior to term judgments.

{defi}

The judgment
{\Gamma}\gg{r}\in\mathbb{I}
is defined to hold when either
r\in\{0,1\}
or
({x}:\mathbb{I})\in\Gamma
;
an equality
{\Gamma}\gg{r}={s}\in\mathbb{I}
is defined to hold when
{\Gamma}\gg{r,s}\in\mathbb{I}
are in the equivalence relation
closure of the constraints appearing in
\Gamma
. The judgments
{\Gamma}\gg{\bm{r}}={\bm{s}}\in\mathbf{I}
and

{\Gamma}\gg{\xi}={\xi^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{constraint}
are defined likewise.

{defi}

We define the well-formed contexts inductively.

\triangleright

{\cdot}={\cdot}\ \mathsf{ctx}
.

\triangleright

{({\Gamma},{a}:{A})}={({\Gamma^{\prime}},{a}:{A^{\prime}})}\ \mathsf{ctx}
when
{\Gamma}={\Gamma^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{ctx}
and

{\Gamma}\gg{A}={A^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
.

\triangleright

{({\Gamma},{x}:\mathbb{I})}={({\Gamma^{\prime}},{x}:\mathbb{I})}\ \mathsf{ctx}
when
{\Gamma}={\Gamma^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{ctx}
.

\triangleright

{({\Gamma},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I})}={({\Gamma^{\prime}},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I})}\ \mathsf{ctx}
when
{\Gamma}={\Gamma^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{ctx}
.

\triangleright

{({\Gamma},{\xi})}={({\Gamma^{\prime}},{\xi})}\ \mathsf{ctx}
when

{\Gamma}={\Gamma^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{ctx}
and
{\Gamma}\gg{\xi}={\xi^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{constraint}
.

A pretype
A
is a
(Kan) type
when it supports the Kan operations, that is, when the operators
\mathsf{coe}

and
\mathsf{hcom}
are well-typed at
A
and satisfy the necessary equations.

{defi}

[Kan types]
Presupposing
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}={A^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, we say
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}={A^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{type}
when the following conditions hold.

\triangleright

For any
{({\Psi^{\prime}},{x}:\mathbb{I})}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
, if
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{r,s}\in\mathbb{I}
and

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{M}={M^{\prime}}\in{A\psi[{r}/{x}]}
, then

–

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\mathsf{coe}_{x.A\psi}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(M)}={\mathsf{coe}_{x.A^{\prime}\psi}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(M^{\prime})}\in{A\psi[{s}/{x}]}
,

–

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\mathsf{coe}_{x.A\psi}^{r\rightsquigarrow r}(M)}={M}\in{A\psi[{r}/{x}]}
,

\triangleright

For any
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
, if
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{r,s}\in\mathbb{I}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\xi_{i}}\ \mathsf{constraint}

for all
i<n
, and

–

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{M}={M^{\prime}}\in{A\psi}

–

{{\Psi^{\prime}},{x}:\mathbb{I}}\Vdash{N_{i}}={N^{\prime}_{j}}\in{A\psi}
for all
i,j<n
,

–

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{M}={N_{i}[{r}/{x}]}\in{A\psi}
for all
i<n
,

then

–

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\mathsf{hcom}_{A\psi}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(M;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}})}={\mathsf{hcom}_{A^{\prime}\psi}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(M^{\prime};\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}^{\prime}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}^{\prime}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}^{\prime}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}^{\prime}}})}\in{A\psi}
,

–

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\mathsf{hcom}_{A\psi}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(M;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}})}={N_{i}[{s}/{x}]}\in{A\psi}
if
\xi_{i}
is true,

–

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\mathsf{hcom}_{A\psi}^{r\rightsquigarrow r}(M;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow x.N_{i}}})}={M}\in{A\psi}
.

The extension of the type judgment to open terms is defined as for the pretype judgment:
{\Gamma}\gg{A}={A^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{type}

holds when
{\Psi}\Vdash{{A}{\gamma}}={{A^{\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}}}\ \mathsf{type}
for all

{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
.

We may also define the open substitution judgment following the pattern of the
instantiation judgment.

{defi}

We define the substitutions
{\Gamma}\gg{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
inductively as follows.

\triangleright

{\Gamma}\gg{\cdot}={\cdot}\in{\cdot}
.

\triangleright

{\Gamma}\gg{({\gamma},{M}/{a})}={({\gamma^{\prime}},{M^{\prime}}/{a})}\in{({\Gamma},{a}:{A})}
when

{\Gamma}\gg{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
and
{\Gamma}\gg{M}={M^{\prime}}\in{A\gamma}
.

\triangleright

{\Gamma}\gg{({\gamma},{r}/{x})}={({\gamma},{r^{\prime}}/{x})}\in{({\Gamma},{x}:\mathbb{I})}
when

{\Gamma}\gg{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
and
{\Gamma}\gg{r}={r^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{I}
.

\triangleright

{\Gamma}\gg{({\gamma},{\bm{r}}/{\bm{x}})}={({\gamma},{\bm{r}^{\prime}}/{\bm{x}})}\in{({\Gamma},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I})}
when

{\Gamma}\gg{\bm{r}}={\bm{r}^{\prime}}\in\mathbf{I}
and
{{\Gamma}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
.

\triangleright

{\Gamma}\gg{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{({\Gamma},{\xi})}
when
{\Gamma}\gg{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
and
\xi\gamma
is
true.

Now that we have laid out the extrapolation of open judgments from a value type system, it remains to
construct a particular type system that will validate the inference rules we presented in

Sections

1
and
2
.

4.4.
Constructing a value type system

We obtain a value type system by a fixed-point construction, first defining the least candidate value type system closed
under our desired type formers and then showing that it constitutes a value type system. To start, we define the
pieces corresponding to each type former. Relative to
[
Ang19
]
, the novelties here are the

\mathsf{Bridge}
- and
\mathsf{Gel}
-types.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\textsc{Bridge}(\tau)\coloneqq\\
\;\{(\Psi,\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.A}(M_{0},M_{1}),\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.A^{\prime}}(M^{\prime}_{0},M^{\prime}_{1}),\varphi)\mid\\
\;\;\;\exists\alpha.\ {{\Psi},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{A}\sim{A^{\prime}}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}\land\mathsf{Coh}(\alpha)\\
\;\;\;{}\land(\forall\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}.\ {\Psi}\Vdash{M_{\varepsilon}}\sim{M^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\in{\alpha[{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}}]})\\
\;\;\;{}\land\varphi=\{(\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.M,\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.M^{\prime})\mid{{\Psi},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{M}\sim{M^{\prime}}\in{\alpha}\land\forall\varepsilon.\ {\Psi}\Vdash{M[{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}}]}\sim{M_{\varepsilon}}\in{\alpha[{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}}]}\}\}\\
\\
\textsc{Gel}(\tau)\coloneqq\\
\;\{(\Psi,\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R),\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A^{\prime}_{0},A^{\prime}_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R^{\prime}),\varphi)\mid\\
\;\;\;\exists\alpha^{0},\alpha^{1},\beta^{(-,-,-,-,-)}.\\
\;\;\;(\forall\varepsilon.\ {{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{x}}}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}}\sim{A_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}\land\mathsf{Coh}(\alpha^{\varepsilon}))\\
\;\;\;{}\land(\forall{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{({\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{x}})}.\ \forall M_{0},M_{1},M_{0}^{\prime},M_{1}^{\prime}.\ (\forall\varepsilon.\ \alpha^{\varepsilon}_{\psi}(M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}))\implies\\
\;\;\;\quad\;\;{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{R[M_{0},M_{1}/a_{0},a_{1}]}\sim{R^{\prime}[M^{\prime}_{0},M^{\prime}_{1}/a_{0},a_{1}]}\downarrow{\beta^{(\psi,M_{0},M_{1},M_{0}^{\prime},M_{1}^{\prime})}}\in{\tau}\\
\;\;\;\quad\;\;{}\land\mathsf{Coh}(\beta^{(\psi,M_{0},M_{1},M_{0}^{\prime},M_{1}^{\prime})}))\\
\;\;\;{}\land\varphi=\{(\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0},M_{1},P),\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M^{\prime}_{0},M^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}))\mid\\
\;\;\;\quad\quad\quad\quad\forall\varepsilon.({{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{x}}}\Vdash{M_{\varepsilon}}\sim{M^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\in{\alpha^{\varepsilon}})\land{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{x}}}\Vdash{P}\sim{P^{\prime}}\in{\beta^{(\mathrm{id},M_{0},M_{1},M_{0}^{\prime},M_{1}^{\prime})}}\}\}\end{array}

Next, we have an operator on candidate value type systems that applies one level of type formers.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}K(\tau)&\coloneqq&\textsc{Bridge}(\tau)\cup\textsc{Gel}(\tau)\cup\cdots\end{array}

Finally, we obtain a least fixed-point
\tau_{0}
of this operator by the Knaster-Tarski fixed-point theorem
[
DP02
, 2.35]
. It is tedious but straightforward to check that this candidate value type system is in
fact a value type system
[
Ang19
, Lemma 4.8]
. To construct a value type system with a universe, we can
repeat the fixed-point construction with the addition of a type
\mathcal{U}
interpreted by the relation
\tau_{0}
,
producing a new type system
\tau_{1}
that is closed under the same type formers as
\tau_{0}
but also contains

\tau_{0}
as a universe. This can be repeated further to produce a hierarchy of value type systems

\tau_{0}\subseteq\tau_{1}\subseteq\tau_{2}\subseteq\cdots
each containing its predecessors as universes; for
our purposes, a single universe is sufficient.

As an immediate consequence of the way the typing judgments are defined, we
have a
canonicity
theorem: any closed well-typed term is guaranteed to evaluate to a value of that
type. In particular, any closed term of natural number type evaluates to a numeral.

4.5.
Building up inference rules

With a value type system in hand, it remains to verify that the judgments are closed under the inference rules
introduced in
Sections

1
and
2
. We go through the typing rules for
\mathsf{Gel}
-types in
detail. The rules for
\mathsf{Bridge}
-types are simpler to verify, as the reduction rules are all “cubically
stable”: they do not depend on the status of any interval term. (In comparison,
\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{r}}(M_{0},M_{1},P)

may be a value or step depending on whether
\bm{r}
is a variable or constant.) The rules for
\mathsf{extent}
do
involve unstable transitions, but require no ideas that are not present in the proofs for
\mathsf{Gel}
-types; in
particular, the
\mathsf{hcom}
reduction for
\mathsf{Gel}
involves
\mathsf{extent}
-like variable capture. The reader may see
[
CH19b
]
for complete proofs of these results.

We rely on the following five lemmas to work with the candidate judgments. These are rephrasings of Lemmas
A.2, A.3, and A.5 from
[
CH18
]
; each follows straightforwardly by unfolding definitions.

Lemma 33

(Coherent type value)
.

Suppose
A,A^{\prime}
are terms. If for every
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\Psi}\in{\Psi}
, either

\tau(\Psi^{\prime},A\psi,A^{\prime}\psi,\alpha_{\psi})
or
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{A\psi}\sim{A^{\prime}\psi}\downarrow{\alpha\psi}\in{\tau}
, then

{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\sim{A^{\prime}}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
.

Lemma 34

(Coherent term value)
.

Suppose
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
and
M,M^{\prime}
are terms. If for every
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\Psi}\in{\Psi}
,
either
\alpha_{\psi}(M\psi,M^{\prime}\psi)
or
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{M\psi}\sim{M^{\prime}\psi}\in{\alpha\psi}
, then

{\Psi}\Vdash{M}\sim{M^{\prime}}\in{\alpha}
.

Lemma 35

(Coherent type expansion)
.

Suppose
A
is a term and
{(A_{\psi})}_{{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}}
is a family of terms such that

A\psi\longmapsto^{*}A_{\psi}
and
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{A_{\psi}}\sim{A_{\mathrm{id}}\psi}\downarrow{\alpha\psi}\in{\tau}
for all

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
. Then
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\sim{A_{\mathrm{id}}}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
.

Lemma 36

(Coherent term expansion)
.

Suppose
{\Psi}\Vdash{A}\downarrow{\alpha}\in{\tau}
,
M
is a term, and
{(M_{\psi})}_{{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}}
is a
family of terms such that
M\psi\longmapsto^{*}M_{\psi}
and
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{M_{\psi}}\sim{M_{\mathrm{id}}\psi}\in{\alpha\psi}
for all

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
. Then
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{M}\sim{M_{\mathrm{id}}}\in{\alpha}
.

Lemma 37

(Evaluation)
.

Suppose
{\Psi}\Vdash{M}={M^{\prime}}\in{A}
. Then
M\Downarrow V
and
M^{\prime}\Downarrow V^{\prime}
with
{\Psi}\Vdash{M=V}={V^{\prime}=M^{\prime}}\in{A}
.

We now check the rules for
\mathsf{Gel}
-types as presented in
Figure

6
. We prove that each rule holds when
the ambient context is an arbitrary interval context
\Psi
. The open rules—for an arbitrary context

\Gamma
—then follow mechanically, as the open type and term judgments are defined by their closed
instantiations.

It is convenient to prove the boundary reduction equations for a type or term former
before
the general
introduction rule; for example, we show first
{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}={A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
and
then
{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}\ \mathsf{pretype}
.

Rule 38

(
Gel-Form-
\partial
)
.

For any
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
,
{\Psi}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, and terms
A_{1-\varepsilon}
,
R
, we have

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}={A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
.

Proof 4.1
.

By Lemma
35
, taking
A_{\psi}\coloneqq A_{\varepsilon}\psi
: we have

\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)\psi\longmapsto A_{\psi}
and

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}\psi}\sim{A_{\varepsilon}\psi}\downarrow{\llbracket{A_{\varepsilon}}\rrbracket\psi}\in{\tau}
for all
\psi
.

As described above, this “closed” principle implies the open rule. Given
{\Gamma}\ \mathsf{ctx}
and

{\Gamma}\gg{A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, we have by definition that
{\Psi}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}\gamma}={A_{\varepsilon}\gamma^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
for all

{\Psi}\Vdash{\gamma}={\gamma^{\prime}}\in{\Gamma}
. Thus
{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)\gamma}={A_{\varepsilon}\gamma^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{pretype}

for all such instantiations by the rule just proven, which means that

{\Gamma}\gg{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}={A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
.

The following lemma gets us part of the way to the formation rule. We also need that the relation for

\mathsf{Gel}
-types is value-coherent and supports the Kan operations; we will return to these later.

Lemma 39

(Gel formation candidate)
.

If we have
{\Psi}\Vdash{\bm{r}}\in\mathbf{I}
,
{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}}={A^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
for
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
, and

{{{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}},{a_{0}}:{A_{0}}},{a_{1}}:{A_{1}}}\gg{R}={R^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, then

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}\sim{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A^{\prime}_{0},A^{\prime}_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R^{\prime})}\downarrow{\gamma}\in{\tau}

with
\gamma
defined on
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
as follows.

\gamma_{\psi}\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\{(\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0},M_{1},P),\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M^{\prime}_{0},M^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}))\mid\\
\;\;\forall\varepsilon.({{\Psi^{\prime}}{\setminus}{\bm{x}}}\Vdash{M_{\varepsilon}}={M^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\in{A\psi})\\
\;\;{}\land{{\Psi^{\prime}}{\setminus}{\bm{x}}}\Vdash{P}={P^{\prime}}\in{R[M_{0},M_{1}/a_{0},a_{1}]}\},&\text{if $\bm{r}\psi=\bm{x}$}\\
\alpha^{\varepsilon}\psi,&\text{if $\bm{r}\psi=\bm{\varepsilon}\in\{\bm{0},\bm{1}\}$}\end{array}\right.

Proof 4.2
.

By Lemma
33
. For every
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
, either

\bm{r}\psi=\bm{x}
for some
\bm{x}
, in which case we have

\tau(\Psi^{\prime},\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)\psi,\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A_{0}^{\prime},A_{1}^{\prime},a_{0}.a_{1}.R^{\prime})\psi,\gamma_{\psi})
by
definition of the value type system, or
\bm{r}\psi=\bm{\varepsilon}\in\{\bm{0},\bm{1}\}
, in which case we have

\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)\psi\sim A_{\varepsilon}\psi\sim A_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\psi\sim\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A^{\prime}_{0},A^{\prime}_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R^{\prime})\psi
by way of
Gel-Form-
\partial
.

Rule 40

(
Gel-Intro-
\partial
)
.

For any
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
,
{\Psi}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, and
{\Psi}\Vdash{M_{\varepsilon}}\in{A_{\varepsilon}}
, and terms

M_{1-\varepsilon}
,
P
, we have
{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(M_{0},M_{1},P)}={M_{\varepsilon}}\in{A_{\varepsilon}}
.

Proof 4.3
.

By Lemma
36
, taking
M_{\psi}\coloneqq M_{\varepsilon}\psi
.

Rule 41

(
Gel-Intro
)
.

If we have
{\Psi}\Vdash{\bm{r}}\in\mathbf{I}
,
{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\Vdash{M_{\varepsilon}}={M^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\in{A_{\varepsilon}}
for
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
,

{{{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}},{a_{0}}:{A_{0}}},{a_{1}}:{A_{1}}}\gg{R}={R^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, and

{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\Vdash{P}={P^{\prime}}\in{R[M_{0},M_{1}/a_{0},a_{1}]}
, then

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{r}}(M_{0},M_{1},P)}\sim{\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{r}}(M^{\prime}_{0},M^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime})}\in{\gamma}
for
\gamma
as in the statement of
Lemma
39
.

Proof 4.4
.

By Lemma
34
, proceeding as in Lemma
39
by cases on

\bm{r}\psi
for each
\psi
: we use the definition of
\gamma
when
\bm{r}\psi
is a variable and

Gel-Intro-
\partial
when
\bm{r}\psi
is a constant.

Lemma 42

(Gel formation pretype)
.

If we have
{\Psi}\Vdash{\bm{r}}\in\mathbf{I}
,
{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}}={A^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
for
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
, and

{{{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}},{a_{0}}:{A_{0}}},{a_{1}}:{A_{1}}}\gg{R}={R^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, then

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}={\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A^{\prime}_{0},A^{\prime}_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R^{\prime})}\ \mathsf{pretype}
.

Proof 4.5
.

A combination of Lemma
39
and
Gel-Intro
, the latter of
which shows that the relation for
\mathsf{Gel}
is value-coherent.

Rule 43

(
Gel-
\beta
)
.

If
{{\Psi},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{P}\in{R[M_{0},M_{1}/a_{0},a_{1}]}
, then

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0},M_{1},P))}={P}\in{R[M_{0},M_{1}/a_{0},a_{1}]}.

Proof 4.6
.

By Lemma
36
: we have

\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0},M_{1},P))\psi\longmapsto P\psi
for all
\psi
.

Rule 44

(
Gel-Elim
)
.

If
{\Psi}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
for
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
,

{{{\Psi},{a_{0}}:{A_{0}}},{a_{1}}:{A_{1}}}\gg{R}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, and

{{\Psi},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{Q}={Q^{\prime}}\in{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},R)}
, then we have the following.

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q)}={\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q^{\prime})}\in{R[Q[{\bm{0}}/{\bm{x}}],Q[{\bm{1}}/{\bm{x}}]/a_{0},a_{1}]}

Proof 4.7
.

For every
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
, we have by Lemma
37
that
Q\psi\Downarrow Q_{\psi}

and
Q^{\prime}\psi\Downarrow Q^{\prime}_{\psi}
for some

{{\Psi^{\prime}},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{Q\psi=Q_{\psi}}={Q^{\prime}_{\psi}=Q^{\prime}\psi}\in{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0}\psi,A_{1}\psi,a_{0}.a_{1}.R\psi)}
. By definition of the relation for
\mathsf{Gel}
-types, we
have
Q_{\psi}=\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0,\psi},M_{1,\psi},P_{\psi})
and

Q_{\psi}^{\prime}=\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0,\psi}^{\prime},M_{1,\psi}^{\prime},P_{\psi}^{\prime})
for some terms such that

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{P_{\psi}}={P^{\prime}_{\psi}}\in{R\psi[M_{0,\psi},M_{1,\psi}/a_{0},a_{1}]}
. By

Gel-Intro-
\partial
and functionality of
R
, it follows that also

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{P_{\psi}}={P^{\prime}_{\psi}}\in{R\psi[Q[{\bm{0}}/{\bm{x}}]\psi,Q[{\bm{1}}/{\bm{x}}]\psi/a_{0},a_{1}]}
. We
have
\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q)\psi\longmapsto^{*}P_{\psi}
for each
\psi
, thus

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q)}={P_{\mathrm{id}}}\in{R[Q[{\bm{0}}/{\bm{x}}],Q[{\bm{1}}/{\bm{x}}]/a_{0},a_{1}]}

by Lemma
36
; likewise,

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q^{\prime})}={P^{\prime}_{\mathrm{id}}}\in{R[Q[{\bm{0}}/{\bm{x}}],Q[{\bm{1}}/{\bm{x}}]/a_{0},a_{1}]}
. We
conclude by transitivity that

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q)=P_{\mathrm{id}}}={P^{\prime}_{\mathrm{id}}=\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q^{\prime})}\in{R[Q[{\bm{0}}/{\bm{x}}],Q[{\bm{1}}/{\bm{x}}]/a_{0},a_{1}]}
.

Rule 45

(
Gel-
\eta
)
.

If
{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{pretype}
for
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
,

{{{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}},{a_{0}}:{A_{0}}},{a_{1}}:{A_{1}}}\gg{R}\ \mathsf{pretype}
, and

{{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{Q}\in{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}
, then we have the following.

{\Psi}\Vdash{Q[{\bm{r}}/{\bm{x}}]}={\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{r}}(Q[{\bm{0}}/{\bm{x}}],Q[{\bm{1}}/{\bm{x}}],\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.Q))}\in{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{r}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}

Proof 4.8
.

By Lemma
37
, we have

{{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{Q}={V}\in{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}
for some
Q\Downarrow V
. By
definition of the relation for
\mathsf{Gel}
-types, we know
V=\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0},M_{1},P)
for some suitably-typed

M_{0}
,
M_{1}
, and
P
. By
Gel-Intro-
\partial
,
Gel-
\beta
, and
Gel-Intro
,
we conclude the following.

{{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{V}={\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(V[{\bm{0}}/{\bm{x}}],V[{\bm{1}}/{\bm{x}}],\mathsf{ungel}(\bm{x}.V))}\in{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}

We can replace
V
with
Q
everywhere in this equation using
Gel-Intro
and

Gel-Elim
. Substituting
\bm{r}
for
\bm{x}
then gives the result.

It only remains to show that
\mathsf{Gel}
-types support the Kan operations. We will go through the proof for

\mathsf{hcom}
; the proof for
\mathsf{coe}
has an identical structure. We will begin by proving reduction lemmas for the
constant and variable cases.

Lemma 46
.

Let
{\Psi}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{type}
for some
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
. If
{\Psi}\Vdash{r,s}\in\mathbb{I}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\Psi}\Vdash{\xi_{i}}\ \mathsf{constraint}
,
{\Psi}\Vdash{Q}\in{A_{\varepsilon}}
,
{{\Psi},{y}:\mathbb{I}}\Vdash{Q_{i}}={Q_{j}}\in{A_{\varepsilon}}
for all

i,j<n
, and
{\Psi}\Vdash{Q}={Q_{i}[{r}/{y}]}\in{A_{\varepsilon}}
for all
i<n
, then

{\Psi}\Vdash{\mathsf{hcom}_{\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{\varepsilon}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(Q;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}})}={\mathsf{hcom}_{A_{\varepsilon}}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(Q;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}})}\in{A_{\varepsilon}}
.

Proof 4.9
.

By Lemma
36
: every substitution instance of the left-hand side steps to the
corresponding instance of the right-hand side, which is well-typed because
A_{\varepsilon}
is Kan.

Lemma 47
.

Let
{\Psi}\Vdash{A_{\varepsilon}}\ \mathsf{type}
for
\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}
and

{{{\Psi},{a_{0}}:{A_{0}}},{a_{1}}:{A_{1}}}\gg{R}\ \mathsf{type}
. Abbreviate

G\coloneqq\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},a_{0}.a_{1}.R)
. For any
{{\Psi},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{r,s}\in\mathbb{I}
,

,

,

{{{\Psi},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}},{y}:\mathbb{I}}\Vdash{Q_{i}}={Q_{j}}\in{G}
for all

{{\Psi},\bm{x}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{\mathsf{hcom}_{G}^{r\rightsquigarrow s}(Q;\mathchoice{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}}{\overarrow@\to{\xi_{i}\hookrightarrow y.Q_{i}}})}={\mathsf{gel}_{\bm{x}}(M_{0,s},M_{1,s},P)}\in{G}

\displaystyle M_{\varepsilon,y}

5.
Formal parametric type theory

While we have anchored our type theory in a computational interpretation, we would also like to use parametric
cubical type theory as a logic for reasoning about other settings. For this reason, we abstract a formal type
theory from the collection of inference rules we have developed in the preceding sections. The proofs of those
inference rules, as given for
\mathsf{Gel}
-types in
Section

4.5
, establish that the
computational interpretation is one model of the formalism. In
Section

6
, we see that the theory can
also be interpreted in cartesian-affine bicubical sets.

We focus on parametric type theory here; for the cubical ingredients, we defer to prior work
[
Ang19
, Appendix
B]
. In the pure parametric case, the theory is defined by the judgments shown in
Figure

9

and their equality counterparts. We take care to ensure our definition constitutes a generalized algebraic
theory (GAT)
[
Car86
]
, using for example explicit substitutions.
2
2

2

We will nevertheless permit ourselves a certain amount of routine syntactic sugar; for one, we will not
fully annotate terms.

Ensuring admissibility of substitution—that every term is equal to one containing no explicit
substitutions—requires some innovation. In particular, the theory presented in
[
BCM15
]
does not
satisfy admissibility of substitution, a consequence of the way rules using interval terms (such as bridge
elimination) are formulated. Rectifying this issue motivates the introduction of the context restriction
operator
{-}{\setminus}{-}
we have already encountered. We present a formulation of context restriction as an
explicit context former characterized as a left adjoint to extension by an interval variable.

{\Gamma}\ \mathsf{ctx}

\Gamma
is a context

{\Gamma}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}

\bm{r}
is a bridge interval term in context
\Gamma

{\Gamma}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}

A
is a type in context
\Gamma

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{A}

M
is a term of type
A
in context
\Gamma

{\Gamma}\vdash{\delta}:{\Delta}

\delta
is a substitution for context
\Delta
in context
\Gamma

Figure 9.
Judgments of formal parametric type theory

We defer serious metatheoretic analysis of the formalism we present, such as normalization or decidability of
equality, to future work.

5.1.
The bridge interval

The main novelty is our treatment of bridge interval restriction. Rather than relying on an operation

{-}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}
on raw contexts—which would destroy the algebraic character of the theory—we treat context
restriction as a primitive context-forming operation.

\frac{}{{\cdot}\ \mathsf{ctx}}\quad\textsc{ctx-nil}\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}}{{{\Gamma}.{A}}\ \mathsf{ctx}}\quad\textsc{ctx-term}\frac{{\Gamma}\ \mathsf{ctx}}{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\ \mathsf{ctx}}\quad\textsc{ctx-$\mathbf{I}$}\frac{{\Gamma}\ \mathsf{ctx}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}}{{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\ \mathsf{ctx}}\quad\textsc{ctx-restrict}

As is usual for ordinary terms, interval terms include variables and are closed under (explicit)
substitutions. We defer the matter of the constants
\bm{0}
and
\bm{1}
for the moment.

\frac{}{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}:\mathbf{I}}\quad\textsc{$\mathbf{I}$-var}\frac{{\Delta}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{\delta}:{\Delta}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{{\bm{r}}[{\delta}]}:\mathbf{I}}\quad\textsc{$\mathbf{I}$-subst}

Restriction is characterized by its relationship with extension by a bridge interval variable. Given an
interval term
{\Gamma}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}
and substitution
{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{\delta}:{\Delta}
, we may build
a substitution
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\delta}.{\bm{r}}}:{{\Delta}.\mathbf{I}}
. Conversely, given

{\Gamma}\vdash{\delta}:{{\Delta}.\mathbf{I}}
, we may project a term
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}[{\delta}]}:\mathbf{I}
and
substitution
{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}[{\delta}]}}\vdash{{\delta}^{\dagger}}:{\Delta}
. This sets up
an adjunction between the category of contexts
\Gamma
and its slice over the bridge interval, which is to say
the category of substitutions
{\Gamma}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}
, with
{-}{\setminus}{-}
as the left adjoint and

{-}.\mathbf{I}
as the right.

\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}\quad{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{\delta}:{\Delta}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{{\delta}.{\bm{r}}}:{{\Delta}.\mathbf{I}}}\quad\textsc{subst-$\mathbf{I}$}\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{\delta}:{{\Delta}.\mathbf{I}}}{{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}[{\delta}]}}\vdash{{\delta}^{\dagger}}:{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{subst-restrict}\\
\frac{{\Delta}\ \mathsf{ctx}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{\delta}:{{\Delta}.\mathbf{I}}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{\delta}={{{\delta}^{\dagger}}.{{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}[{\delta}]}}:{{\Delta}.\mathbf{I}}}\quad\textsc{subst-eq-$\mathbf{I}$}\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}\quad{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{\delta}:{\Delta}}{{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{\delta}={{({\delta}.{\bm{r}})}^{\dagger}}:{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{subst-eq-restrict}

These rules induce a functorial action by interval extension,

{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{{\delta}^{\mathbf{I}}\coloneqq{({\delta}\mathbin{\circ}{{\mathsf{id}}^{\dagger}})}.{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}}:{{\Delta}.\mathbf{I}}
, as well as an action
by restriction,

{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{{\bm{r}}[{\delta}]}}\vdash{{\delta}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}\coloneqq{({{\mathsf{id}}.{\bm{r}}}\mathbin{\circ}{\delta})}^{\dagger}}:{{\Delta}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}
. Using these, we
additionally require that the correspondence is natural.

\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{\delta}:{\Delta}\quad{\Xi}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}\quad{{\Xi}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{\gamma}:{\Gamma}}{{\Xi}\vdash{{({\delta}\mathbin{\circ}{\gamma})}.{\bm{r}}}={{{\delta}^{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbin{\circ}{({\gamma}.{\bm{r}})}}:{{\Delta}.\mathbf{I}}}\quad\textsc{subst-$\mathbf{I}$-natural}\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{\delta}:{{\Delta}.\mathbf{I}}\quad{\Xi}\vdash{\gamma}:{\Gamma}}{{{\Xi}.{\setminus}{{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}[{{\delta}\mathbin{\circ}{\gamma}}]}}\vdash{{({\delta}\mathbin{\circ}{\gamma})}^{\dagger}}={{{\delta}^{\dagger}}\mathbin{\circ}{({\gamma}{\setminus}{{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}[{\delta}]})}}:{\Delta}}\quad\textsc{subst-restrict-natural}

The structural laws
and constants are then given as generating substitutions (together with the expected equations between them,
such as
{\mathsf{p}_{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbin{\circ}{{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{I}}}=\mathsf{id}
and naturality laws).

\frac{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{I}}}:{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}}\quad\textsc{subst-face}\frac{}{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{\mathsf{p}_{\mathbf{I}}}:{\Gamma}}\quad\textsc{subst-degen}\frac{{\Gamma}\ \mathsf{ctx}}{{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{\mathsf{ex}_{\mathbf{I}}}:{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}.\mathbf{I}}}\quad\textsc{subst-exchange}

Note that the existence of a substitution
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{I}}}:{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}
is
slightly stronger than the existence of a term
{\Gamma}\vdash{\overline{{\bm{\bm{\varepsilon}}}_{\mathbf{I}}}}:\mathbf{I}
; the
latter would only give us a substitution

{\Gamma}\vdash{{\mathsf{id}}.{\overline{{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{I}}}}}:{{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}[{\overline{{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{I}}}}]}}.\mathbf{I}}
.

We note that the rules for
\mathbf{I}
we have presented so far are consistent with an interpretation by a
structural interval, in which case context restriction would be the identity function. It is not until we
introduce rules for
\mathsf{extent}
and
\mathsf{Gel}
that the structural interval ceases to model the theory.

On the cubical side, we can treat path interval variables in the same way as term variables. However, we also
need the principle that bridge and path variables can be exchanged.

\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{\delta}:{\Delta}\quad{\Delta}\vdash{r}:\mathbb{I}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{{\delta}.{r}}:{{\Delta}.\mathbb{I}}}\quad\textsc{subst-$\mathbb{I}$}\frac{}{{{\Gamma}.\mathbb{I}}\vdash{\mathsf{p}_{\mathbb{I}}}:{\Gamma}}\quad\textsc{subst-proj-$\mathbb{I}$}\frac{{\Gamma}\ \mathsf{ctx}}{{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}.\mathbb{I}}\vdash{\mathsf{ex}_{\mathbb{I}\mathbf{I}}}:{{{\Gamma}.\mathbb{I}}.\mathbf{I}}}\quad\textsc{subst-$\mathbb{I}\mathbf{I}$}

The substitution
\mathsf{ex}_{\mathbb{I}\mathbf{I}}
serves to invert the substitution

{{{\Gamma}.\mathbb{I}}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{{{\mathsf{p}_{\mathbb{I}}}^{\mathbf{I}}}.{{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbb{I}}}[{\mathsf{p}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}}:{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}.\mathbb{I}}
,
and expresses that path terms are always apart from bridge terms. Besides this principle, the cubical and
parametric sides of the theory only interact via the allowance for bridge constraints in
\mathsf{hcom}
terms and
the inclusion of rules for computing Kan operations in
\mathsf{Bridge}
- and
\mathsf{Gel}
-types, which we may formulate
following the operational semantics shown in
Figure

2
.

5.2.
Type and term formers

With the judgmental infrastructure in place, it is fairly straightforward to translate the computational type
formers introduced in
Section

2
to the formal setting. We describe the rules for
\mathsf{Bridge}
-types
here; rules for
\mathsf{Gel}
-types and
\mathsf{extent}
may be found in
Appendix

A
. The formation,
introduction, and elimination rules for
\mathsf{Bridge}
-types follow exactly the pattern of Figure
4
.

\frac{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{0}}:{{A}[{{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{1}}:{{A}[{{\bm{1}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M_{0},M_{1})}\ \mathsf{type}}\frac{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{M}:{A}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{}.M}:{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}({M}[{{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{I}}}],{M}[{{\bm{1}}_{\mathbf{I}}}])}}\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}\quad{{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{M_{0}}:{{A}[{{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{M_{1}}:{{A}[{{\bm{1}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{P}:{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M_{0},M_{1})}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{P\bm{@}{\bm{r}}}:{{A}[{{\mathsf{id}}.{\bm{r}}}]}}

It is the elimination rule—along with the rules for
\mathsf{extent}
and
\mathsf{Gel}
-types—that necessitates the
introduction of the interval restriction operator. In
[
BCM15
]
, bridge elimination is instead
described by a rule of the following kind.

\frac{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{0}}:{{A}[{{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{1}}:{{A}[{{\bm{1}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{P}:{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M_{0},M_{1})}}{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{\mathsf{app}(P)}:{A}}\quad\textsc{tm-app-bcm}

This form of elimination is inter-derivable with our own: one may set

P\bm{@}{\bm{r}}\coloneqq{\mathsf{app}(P)}[{{\mathsf{id}}.{\bm{r}}}]
or conversely

\mathsf{app}(P)\coloneqq{P}[{{\mathsf{id}}^{\dagger}}]\bm{@}{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}
. However, the
[
BCM15
]
rule produces a formalism in which substitution is not admissible, that is, a theory in
which not every term is equal to one containing no use of the
{-}[{-}]
operator. Given
P
as in the
rule and a substitution
{\Delta}\vdash{\gamma}:{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}
, there is no way to reduce the term

{\mathsf{app}(P)}[{\gamma}]
unless it happens that
\Delta={\Delta^{\prime}}.\mathbf{I}
and
\gamma={\gamma^{\prime}}^{\mathbf{I}}
for
some
{\Delta^{\prime}}\vdash{\gamma^{\prime}}:{\Gamma}
, in which case

{\mathsf{app}(P)}[{\gamma}]=\mathsf{app}({P}[{\gamma^{\prime}}])
. By contrast, we may reduce a term

{(P\bm{@}{\bm{r}})}[{\gamma}]
using the functorial action of restriction, as prescribed by the rule
below.

\frac{{\Delta}\vdash{\gamma}:{\Gamma}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}\quad{{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{M_{0}}:{{A}[{{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{M_{1}}:{{A}[{{\bm{1}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\vdash{P}:{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M_{0},M_{1})}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{{(P\bm{@}{\bm{r}})}[{\gamma}]}={{P}[{{\gamma}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}]\bm{@}{{\bm{r}}[{\gamma}]}}:{{{A}[{{\mathsf{id}}.{\bm{r}}}]}[{\gamma}]}}

Finally, the
\beta
-,
\eta
-, and boundary rules for
\mathsf{Bridge}
-types can be expressed as follows. Note that these
rules respectively make use of the unit

{\Gamma}\vdash{{\mathsf{id}}.{\bm{r}}}:{{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}.\mathbf{I}}
and counit

{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}.{\setminus}{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}}\vdash{{\mathsf{id}}^{\dagger}}:{\Gamma}
of the adjunction between

{-}{\setminus}{-}
and
{-}.\mathbf{I}
.

\frac{{\Gamma}\vdash{\bm{r}}:\mathbf{I}\quad{{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{{{\Gamma}.{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{M}:{A}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{\lambda{}.{M}\bm{@}{\bm{r}}}={{M}[{{\mathsf{id}}.{\bm{r}}}]}:{{A}[{{\mathsf{id}}.{\bm{r}}}]}}\frac{{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{0}}:{{A}[{{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{1}}:{{A}[{{\bm{1}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{P}:{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M_{0},M_{1})}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{P}={\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{}.{P}[{{\mathsf{id}}^{\dagger}}]\bm{@}{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}}:{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M_{0},M_{1})}}\frac{\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}\quad{{\Gamma}.\mathbf{I}}\vdash{A}\ \mathsf{type}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{0}}:{{A}[{{\bm{0}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{1}}:{{A}[{{\bm{1}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}\quad{\Gamma}\vdash{P}:{\mathsf{Bridge}_{A}(M_{0},M_{1})}}{{\Gamma}\vdash{{P}[{{{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{I}}}^{\dagger}}]\bm{@}{{\mathsf{q}_{\mathbf{I}}}[{{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}}={M_{\varepsilon}}:{{A}[{{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\mathbf{I}}}]}}\par

6.
A semantics in bicubical sets

We now describe a second semantics for the formal type theory of
Section

5
in a presheaf category of

bicubical sets
, adapting Angiuli et al.’s presheaf semantics for cubical type theory
[
ABC+19
]
.

{defi}

We define the
cartesian-affine bicube category

\square_{\mathit{ca}}
to have as objects interval contexts
\Psi

and as morphisms interval substitutions
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
, as specified in Definition
4.1
.

Remark 49
.

The category
\square_{\mathit{ca}}
is equivalent to a product
\square_{\mathit{c}}\times\square_{\mathit{a}}
of two
cube categories
, the

cartesian cube category

\square_{\mathit{c}}
consisting of path interval contexts and the
affine cube
category

\square_{\mathit{a}}
consisting of bridge interval contexts.

The presheaf category
[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
is the category of contravariant functors from
\square_{\mathit{ca}}
to
\mathbf{Set}
,
meaning that its objects are families of sets indexed by interval contexts with transition maps for each
interval substitution. This parallels the situation in the computational interpretation, where types are given
meaning by families of relations indexed by such contexts. We use
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}
(hiragana ‘yo’) to denote the
Yoneda embedding
\square_{\mathit{ca}}\to[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
.

Remark 50
.

Bernardy, Coquand, and Moulin instead interpret their type theory in a category of
refined presheaves

on
\square_{\mathit{a}}

[
BCM15
]
. Roughly, a refined presheaf is a
\Psi
-indexed family where for each

\Psi\in\square_{\mathit{a}}
, we have not merely a set but a
\Psi
-set
, a family of sets indexed by sub-contexts

\Psi^{\prime}\subseteq\Psi
. This refinement is used to validate the equivalents in their setting of equations

\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},R)}=R
and

C=\lambda^{\mathbf{I}}\bm{x}.\mathsf{Gel}_{\bm{x}}(A_{0},A_{1},\mathsf{Bridge}_{\bm{x}.C\bm{@}{\bm{x}}})
, as mentioned in

Section

2.4
. When we build parametric type theory on a cubical base, we no longer need these
equations to hold exactly, as we can prove they hold up to a path using univalence (Theorem
8
).

6.1.
Judgments and cubical type theory

We recall the presheaf interpretation of the judgments of cubical type theory developed in
[
CCHM15
,
ABC+19
]
,
which draw on earlier presheaf interpretations of dependent type theory
[
Hof97
]
.

{defi}

A
semantic context
is a presheaf
G\in[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
; a
semantic substitution
between
contexts
G^{\prime},G
is a presheaf morphism (
i.e.
, natural transformation)
\alpha:G^{\prime}\to G
.

{defi}

A
semantic pretype
over a context
G\in[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
is a presheaf
T\in[(\int G)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
over the
category of elements
\int G
, which is to say the following data:

\triangleright

for every
\Psi\in\square_{\mathit{ca}}
and
g\in G(\Psi)
, a set
T(\Psi,g)
;

\triangleright

for every
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
and
g\in G(\Psi)
, a map

T(\psi):T(\Psi^{\prime},G(\psi)(g))\to T(\Psi,g)
.

{defi}

A
semantic element

t
of a pretype
T
in context
G
is a family of elements

t(\Psi,g)\in T(\Psi,g)
indexed by
\Psi\in\square_{\mathit{ca}}
and
g\in G(\Psi)
such that

T(\psi)(t(\Psi,g))=t(\Psi^{\prime},G(\psi)(g))
for every
{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
and
g\in G(\Psi)
.

A semantic
type
is then a pretype equipped with coercion and homogeneous composition operators
implementing the rules shown in Figure
2
. We give the definition of coercion operator here and
leave it to the reader to infer the corresponding notion of
homogeneous composition operator
.

{defi}

Given a pretype
T
over
G
, a
coercion operator

c
for
T
is a family of elements as follows: for
every
\Psi\in\square_{\mathit{ca}}
, interval terms
{\Psi}\Vdash{r,s}\in\mathbb{I}
, element
g\in G({\Psi},{x}:\mathbb{I})
,
and
t\in T(\Psi,G({\mathsf{id}_{\Psi}},{r}/{x})(g))
, we require an element

c(\Psi,r,s,g,t)\in T(\Psi,G({\mathsf{id}_{\Psi}},{s}/{x})(g))
. We ask that these satisfy the
following properties.

\triangleright

T(\psi)(c(\Psi,r,s,g,t))=c(\Psi^{\prime},r\psi,s\psi,G(\psi)(g),T(\psi)(t))
for every

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
.

\triangleright

c(\Psi,r,r,g,t)=t
.

{defi}

A
semantic type

(T,c,h)
over
G
is a triple consisting of a semantic pretype
T
over
G
with
coercion and homogeneous composition operators
c
and
h
.

Remark 51
.

A semantic substitution
\alpha:G^{\prime}\to G
acts on types and terms over
G
by reindexing; we write
\alpha^{*}T

and
\alpha^{*}t
for the action on types and terms respectively.

{defi}

A
semantic interval term
over
G
is a presheaf morphism
r:G\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{({x}:\mathbb{I})}
. A

semantic constraint
is a morphism
r:G\to\Omega_{\mathit{dec}}
where
\Omega_{\mathit{dec}}
is the
decidable
subobject classifier
in
[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
, which classifies monomorphisms
m:H^{\prime}\rightarrowtail H
in

[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
such that
m(\Psi)
has decidable image for all
\Psi
.

Angiuli
et al.
’s
[
ABC+19
, Theorem 1]
shows that cartesian cubical type theory can be interpreted using
these semantic judgments in any presheaf category whose base category contains a suitably structured interval
object.

Proposition 52
.

[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
interprets cubical type theory with an infinite hierarchy of univalent universes, each
closed under dependent function and product types,
\mathsf{Path}
-types, and
\mathsf{V}
-types.

Proof 6.1
.

By Angiuli
et al.
’s Theorem 1
[
ABC+19
]
. The formulation of cartesian cubical type theory given there is
slightly different from our own (for example taking
\mathsf{com}
rather than
\mathsf{coe}
and
\mathsf{hcom}
as primitive),
but not in any essential way.

We note that the statement of the theorem in
[
ABC+19
]
requires that the base category is closed under
finite products, which is not the case for
\square_{\mathit{ca}}
: the cartesian product of the contexts

(\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})
and
(\bm{\bm{y}}:\mathbf{I})
does not exist. However, the proof only actually requires that
the product functor
-\times({x}:\mathbb{I})
exists, and this is indeed the case in
\square_{\mathit{ca}}
.

6.2.
Bridge interval and restriction

We now turn to the parametric side of the theory. As with the path interval, we interpret bridge interval
terms in a context
G
as morphisms
\bm{r}:G\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{(\bm{x}:\mathbf{I})}
. To interpret bridge interval context
extension and restriction, we observe that we have an adjunction between
\square_{\mathit{ca}}
and its slice category over
the affine interval
(\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})
. Note that elements of this slice category consist of contexts
\Psi

paired with bridge interval terms
{\Psi}\Vdash{\bm{r}}\in\mathbf{I}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square_{\mathit{ca}}/(\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square_{\mathit{ca}}\phantom{/(\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{Res}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{Ext}}

The right adjoint
\mathit{Ext}
sends a context
\Psi
to the extended context
({\Psi},\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})
with its
canonical projection
{{\Psi},\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I}}\Vdash{\bm{x}}\in\mathbf{I}
. The left adjoint is interval restriction: it sends
a pair
(\Psi,\bm{r})
to the restricted context
{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}
defined here as in Section
2.1
.

\displaystyle{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Psi\quad\text{if $\bm{\varepsilon}\in\{\bm{0},\bm{1}\}$}

\displaystyle{({\Psi},{y}:\mathbb{I})}{\setminus}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{x}}},{y}:\mathbb{I}

\displaystyle{({\Psi},\bm{y}:\mathbf{I})}{\setminus}{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Psi&\text{if $\bm{x}=\bm{y}$}\\
{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{x}}},\bm{y}:\mathbf{I}&\text{if $\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}$}\end{array}\right.

This adjunction in the base category induces, among other things, the following pair of adjoint functors
between the presheaf category and its slice. We implicitly use the equivalence

[(\square_{\mathit{ca}}/\Psi)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]\simeq[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]/\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\Psi}
between presheaves on slice categories and slices over
representables.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]/\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{(\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathit{Res}}_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{Res}^{*}\cong\mathit{Ext}_{!}}

Here
\mathit{Res}^{*}
is precomposition with
\mathit{Res}
, while
\mathit{Res}_{!}
and
\mathit{Ext}_{!}
are each defined by
left Kan extension. Both
\mathit{Ext}_{!}
and
\mathit{Res}^{*}
are left adjoint to
\mathit{Ext}^{*}
, so are
necessarily isomorphic. As for
\mathit{Res}_{!}
, it may be explicitly calculated as the following coend.

\mathit{Res}_{!}(G,\bm{r})(\Psi)=\int^{{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\bm{s}}:\mathbf{I}}\{g\in G(\Psi^{\prime})\mid\bm{r}(\Psi^{\prime})(g)=\bm{s}\}\times\{\psi\mid{\Psi}\Vdash{\psi}\in{{\Psi^{\prime}}{\setminus}{\bm{s}}}\}

For our purposes, however, it is only necessary to know that the extensions
{\mathit{Res}}_{!}
and
\mathit{Ext}_{!}

apply the base functors on representables, that is, that

{\mathit{Res}}_{!}(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\Psi},\bm{r})\cong\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{({\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}})}
and

\mathit{Ext}_{!}(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\Psi})\cong\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{({\bm{x}}/{\bm{x}})}:\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{({\Psi},\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{(\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}
; this is a general property of Kan extensions. Henceforth we write
{G}\otimes\mathbf{I}
for
the object part of
\mathit{Ext}_{!}G
and
\mathit{var}(G):{G}\otimes\mathbf{I}\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{(\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}
for the
associated projection.

We use
\mathit{Res}_{!}
to interpret the type-theoretic restriction of a context by an interval term, likewise

{-}\otimes\mathbf{I}
to interpret extension by an interval variable and
\mathit{var}(G)
for the variable rule. The
isomorphism between hom-sets given by the adjunction
\mathit{Res}_{!}\dashv\mathit{Ext}_{!}
implements the substitution
constructors
subst-
\mathbf{I}
and
subst-restrict
. The structural rules for the bridge
interval derive from natural transformations in the base category via the action of
{(-)}_{!}
; for example, the
endpoint transformation
\bm{\varepsilon}:\mathit{Id}\to\pi\circ\mathit{Ext}
defined by

{\Psi}\Vdash{\bm{\varepsilon}(\Psi)\coloneqq({\mathsf{id}_{\Psi}},{\bm{\varepsilon}}/{\bm{x}})}\in{({\Psi},\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}

induces a corresponding transformation
\bm{\varepsilon}_{!}:\mathit{Id}\to({-}\otimes\mathbf{I})
in the presheaf category
interpreting the endpoint substitution
subst-face
.

6.3.
Type and term formers

To interpret the rules for forming types and terms—
\mathsf{Bridge}
-types,
\mathsf{Gel}
-types, and
\mathsf{extent}
—it
is useful to observe that the semantic judgments, like the computational ones in
Section

4
, are
determined by their instantiations at interval contexts (
i.e.
, representables). For example, a semantic type

T
in context
G
is determined by the types
g^{*}T
for
g:\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\Psi}\to G
: recalling that the Yoneda
lemma identifies morphisms
g:\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\Psi}\to G
with elements
g\in G(\Psi)
, we have as we have

T(\Psi,g)=(g^{*}T)(\Psi,\mathsf{id}_{\Psi})
. Conversely, if we have a family of types
T_{g}
over
\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\Psi}

for every
g:\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\Psi}\to G
such that
{(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\psi})}^{*}T_{g}=T_{g\circ\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{7.97224pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 0.79727pt}}{\raisebox{7.97224pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 0.79727pt}}{\raisebox{5.58054pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 0.79727pt}}{\raisebox{3.98611pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 0.79727pt}}}{\psi}}
for all

{\Psi^{\prime}}\Vdash{\psi}\in{\Psi}
, then this determines a type
T
over
G
: take

T(\Psi,g)\coloneqq T_{g}(\Psi,\mathsf{id}_{\Psi})
. A similar principle applies to terms.

The upshot is that we may verify that rules hold in an arbitrary context by showing they hold (naturally) in
any interval context, as we did for the computational interpretation in Section
4.5
.
In the restricted case we may take advantage of the characterizations

{\mathit{Res}}_{!}(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\Psi},\bm{r})\cong\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{({\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}})}
and

\mathit{Ext}_{!}(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{\Psi})\cong\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{({\bm{x}}/{\bm{x}})}:\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{6.99997pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}{\raisebox{5.0pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.00006pt}}}{({\Psi},\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.00002pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip , saving us from formal reasoning with the general Kan extension.

Theorem 53
.

[\square_{\mathit{ca}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
is closed under
\mathsf{Bridge}
-pretypes.

Proof 6.2
.

Per the argument above, we narrow our attention without loss of generality to the cases where the ambient
context is representable.

\triangleright

Formation.

Let a semantic pretype
T
in context
{\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{7.15547pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{5.11108pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}}{\Psi}}\otimes\mathbf{I}\cong\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{7.15547pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{5.11108pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}}{({\Psi},\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}
be given
together with endpoint elements

of

\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{7.15547pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{5.11108pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}}{\Psi}

in context

Elimination.

To interpret application, we assume now that we have some

\bm{r}:\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{7.15547pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{5.11108pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}}{\Psi}\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{7.15547pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{5.11108pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}}{({\Psi},\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}
and that the pretype
T
lies in context

{\mathit{Res}_{!}(\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{7.15547pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{5.11108pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}}{\Psi},\bm{r})}\otimes\mathbf{I}\cong\mathord{\mathchoice{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{10.22217pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{7.15547pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}{\raisebox{5.11108pt}{\includegraphics[quiet,draft,height,keepaspectratio]{graphics/yo-serif.pdf}\hskip 1.02228pt}}}{({{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}},\bm{\bm{x}}:\mathbf{I})}
. Given

\mathit{app}^{\mathbf{I}}(u)(\Psi^{\prime},\psi)\coloneqq T({\bm{r}\psi}/{\bm{x}})(u({\Psi^{\prime}}{\setminus}{\bm{r}\psi},{\psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}))

Here
{{\Psi^{\prime}}{\setminus}{\bm{r}\psi}}\Vdash{{\psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}\in{{\Psi}{\setminus}{\bm{r}}}
is the functorial
action of restriction on
\psi

; applying
T({\bm{r}\psi}/{\bm{x}})
thus gives an element of
T(\Psi^{\prime},\psi)
.

We leave it to the reader to check that these definitions are natural and that the
\beta
-,

\eta
-, and boundary rules are satisfied.
